A Skier Broke Both Legs in an Avalanche, Then Survived More Than 36 Hours in a Crevasse

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Members of France’s CRS Savoie mountain-rescue team search a snow-covered slope near Pointe Sommeiller in the French Alps after a skier was swept into a crevasse by an avalanche on November 8, 2026. (Photo: CRS Savoie)

A backcountry skier in France is lucky to be alive after an avalanche left him trapped in a crevasse for more than 36 hours with both legs broken.

The avalanche, which occurred in the French Alps, swept the skier into the crevasse on November 8, according to a Facebook statement from the French national police’s CRS Savoie mountain-rescue team. The skier crawled the length of a football field along a passage inside the crevasse, searching for a way out, before rescuers found him alive on November 9.

The 39-year-old, whose name has not been released, was on a solo backcountry ski trip near the French-Italian border. He was headed for the summit of Pointe Sommeiller, a 10,932-foot peak known for its steep, technical terrain, according to the French Alpine Club, when the avalanche hit.

The peak is located on the France-Italy border, 100 miles east of Grenoble.

Details of the rescue were shared in the official Facebook post, and also in a news story published by French newspaper Le Dauphiné Libére.

According to these reports, the skier left the Alpine village of Bramans on November 7 and spent the night at a backcountry mountain hut before setting out the following morning to climb Pointe Sommeiller.

At the base of a steep, snow-filled chute, he strapped his skis to his pack and began booting up the slope in crampons. His weight triggered a slab avalanche that swept him into a crevasse, fracturing his femur and breaking his other lower leg. Stranded without cell service, he ditched his gear and began crawling down a sloping passage inside the crevasse, hunting for an exit to the surface.

He eventually reached a chamber roughly 50 feet beneath the snow, but the slope leading out was too steep to climb with broken legs, Major Yoann Haberey of the CRS Alpes mountain-rescue unit told Le Dauphiné Libéré.

A concerned relative reported the skier missing late on November 8, prompting a search that continued until approximately 2 A.M., after the risk of another avalanche forced rescuers to suspend operations.

Crews returned the following morning, when a helicopter spotted signs of the avalanche. Search teams began probing and digging through the snow, gradually uncovering the man’s skis and backpack.

After more than five hours of excavation, rescuers finally heard his voice coming from roughly 50 feet beneath them.

The man was conscious but suffering from hypothermia when crews reached him. They extracted him using a stretcher and winch system, lowered him roughly 650 vertical feet down the mountain, and evacuated him by helicopter to a hospital in southeastern France.

The operation involved 22 officers from France’s CRS mountain-rescue unit, six military police rescuers, and three helicopters.

“He really is a miracle survivor,” Haberey told Le Dauphiné Libéré. “They were forecasting snowfall overnight, so after that, we weren’t going to go back.”

Following the rescue, the CRS Savoie mountain-rescue team urged people heading into the mountains to adjust their plans to current conditions, prepare their routes and equipment, and know when to turn back.

“The mountain will always be there tomorrow,” the rescuers wrote.

Where to catch Bloodgill in DREADMOOR

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

While attempting to fix all tuning forks in DREADMOOR, Malcolm will meet the enigmatic Father Matthias, who will task him with catching two Bloodgill fish if he wants to finish his quest.

Contrary to what you might imagine, you won’t be able to find Bloodgill spawns close to Father Matthias’ island in DREADMOOR. Instead, you can catch the fish by heading to the spot marked below, located south of The Slough’s Trading Post. The fish will become way more common after you reach the Big Hollow, though, and will be easy to spot close to the post’s new location.

Manager of the season is a one-horse race, Man United have taken incompetence to new levels and Newcastle to go down? Our experts' verdict on the Premier League so far

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Published: 15:31 EST, 10 November 2026 | Updated: 15:32 EST, 10 November 2026

With ten games of the Premier League season gone, it's time for another breather as the second international break rolls around. Here's our experts' verdict on the season so far...

Ronald Araujo. The Uruguayan on loan at Liverpool from Barcelona has gone some way to solving a huge problem for Andoni Iraola at right-back. IAN LADYMAN

Pascal Gross – epitome of the Brighton formula. The first signing when they reached the Premier League now back for his second spell. Supreme confidence in the model shines through him. He is understated and over delivering at 35. MATT BARLOW

It’s A Relief To Go Back To The Simple Joy Of Ocarina Of Time After A Decade Of Being Overwhelmed By Breath Of The Wild

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the WildThe Legend of Zelda: Ocarina Of TimeThe Legend of Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom

My friends, put the tomatoes down. I havenât even gotten to speak yet. I, like most people who own a Nintendo Switch 2 and are killing time until Grand Theft Auto 6 launches next week, am playing The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time, and Iâm having a pretty good time going back to a game that was once an elementary school obsession for me, especially because itâs been a long time since I really felt a constant sense of progression in a Zelda game after a decade of Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom.

Yeah, the game is incredibly simple by modern standards, and Nintendo has done a lot to ensure that by making the game demonstrably easier in dozens of small ways that add up, but I think that relative simplicity is what draws me to it. Iâve been chipping away at the game in short sessions, usually finishing a temple or two before putting it down for the rest of the day, and the tangible feeling of momentum is what keeps me coming back to it just about every other evening at this point.

Over the years, especially with the rise of open-world games, I have identified that I am a goal-oriented person when it comes to how a video game can hold my attention. Thereâs just so much competition for whatever spare hours I have that if the bulk of my time playing a game is spent getting to the things I care about, I can lose interest fairly quickly. I want to see where the story goes, I want to reach the end of a quest line, and I loathe tedious open-world traversal more than most video game activities.Â

I canât entirely avoid open-world games in 2026, but if a game doesnât have a quick or engaging way to get around a giant map, it actively deters me from playing it. I often cite the Infamous series, with all its superpowered movement and interesting environmental interactions, as a great example of a game that makes its open-world map tolerable to me. A car or a horse is probably not going to cut it. There are exceptions from time to time, such as Cyberpunk 2077âs Night City entrancing me so much that Iâd gladly take in the view as I drove around on my motorcycle. But if I can help it, I want to spend my limited gaming time on things that arenât simply moving from one side of a giant space to another.

Breath of the Wild and its excellent sequel, Tears of the Kingdom do a lot to break up the tedium of simply moving through a space. The survival sim-esque friction and gizmo-building mechanics are brilliant evolutions of the original Zelda formula, and Nintendoâs ability to still draw water from the well of a decades-old franchise is commendable. I really enjoyed my time with both games, despite them grinding against a lot of my ingrained gaming sensibilities. The puzzles in the shrines, the constant feeling of discovery in its giant open world, and the colorful casts of weirdos and heroes you meet throughout the games maintain all that same Zelda spirit, but with a meaningful expansion of what a Zelda game looks like.Â

But man, is it a relief to go back to Ocarina of Time. I want puzzle-solving and discovery that I can get to quickly, that doesnât need me to meaningfully prep a survival bag so I can trek to the other side of the map while hoping my melee weapons can last me the whole trip, and what Ocarina is delivering feels downright breezy. Iâm sorry, Iâm sorry. I know itâs regressive of me to say Iâd like for the series to strip away all the cool things itâs done in the past nine years, but I havenât had a Zelda experience that feels like this in so long. The top-down 2D games are similar (at least those that came after the pretty open-ended and opaque original game), but they donât have the targeting and swordplay of the postâOcarina of Time era that rounds out the vision I have of what makes a 3D Zelda game.

I donât really hold any illusions that Zelda is going to go back to the structure of Ocarina of Time, but it is refreshing to be handed the tools I need to accomplish things without worrying that my weapon is going to break from battle damage or that Iâll run out of some vital consumable before I reach my goal. The Legend of Zelda is gonna be fine no matter where it goes moving forward, but for as dated and small in scope as Ocarina of Time might feel compared to Tears of the Kingdom, it sure does remind me that there was once a time when I didnât feel like I was fighting against the elements just to get to where I wanted to go.

What happens to mathematics when AI becomes an oracle?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Mathematician Steven Strogatz discusses his new book, Big Math: The Hidden Codes That Run Our World.

Our modern world runs on math, from social media algorithms, package delivery, and weather prediction to medical imaging and machine learning. The rise of computers helped fuel its dominance, enabling us to work with increasingly large numbers that would otherwise be beyond the capacity of our finite human brains. But with the rise of large language models, AI chatbots, and, soon, quantum computing, so-called “big math” is beginning to outstrip its creators entirely. What that means, and how we might adapt, is the focus of a new book, Big Math: The Hidden Codes That Run Our World, by applied mathematicians Steven Strogatz and Alex Townsend.

Strogatz is perhaps best known in his field for his co-authorship of a seminal 1998 paper with Duncan Watts on the collective dynamics of small-world networks. He has also studied chaos theory as applied to physics, the geometry of supercoiled DNA, and the biological collective oscillators, such as swarms of synchronously flashing fireflies and crickets that chirp in unison. In 2005, Strogatz extended his study of synchronicity to the crowds that caused the wobbling of London’s Millennium Bridge.

An award-winning communicator, Strogatz wrote three series of columns on mathematics for The New York Times in 2010, 2012, and 2025 and hosted a podcast for Quanta magazine from 2020–2021. He has written four popular books: Sync (2003), The Calculus of Friendship (2009), The Joy of X (2012), and the New York Times bestseller Infinite Powers (2019).

This time around, Strogatz has found a well-matched co-author in Townsend, a younger colleague recently hired at Cornell who studies numerical analysis and deep learning. As senior faculty, Strogatz was assigned to mentor Townsend, and the two men began regularly lunching together in Risley Hall. Strogatz was intrigued by the popular book potential of Townsend’s course on the top 20 algorithms of the 20th century and suggested co-authorship. That concept slowly morphed into a book about big matrix calculations, and then into what became Big Math.

“There are so many books about pure math, as if computers didn’t exist,” Strogatz told Ars. “Alex and I are both very applied mathematicians. We thought there should be a book on computers and math, especially the class of really big problems with billions of variables or parameters that you just can’t approach in your head. A lot of the math we’ve had for centuries perfectly scales up to this computer moment. What the abacus did for arithmetic, the fangcheng (“rectangular array”) board did for systems of linear equations. It was a matrix you could manipulate with your hand and bamboo sticks. So this externalizing of math outside of our heads into our tools is a 2,000-year-old idea.”

“The mathematician in each of us will always want to know more,” Strogatz and Townsend wrote. “The human being must insist that knowledge serves something larger than itself. That might be the work of our century: letting our power grow without losing our soul. Big Math keeps getting bigger. So must we.”

Ars caught up with Strogatz to learn more.

Ars Technica: Let’s start by defining “big math,” because it’s such a broad umbrella term.

Steven Strogatz: Operationally, big math is “big” relative to human cognitive capacity. So it can be surprisingly small and yet big. We also wanted to expand it to things that people would think of as computational engineering—in the building of the Burj Khalifa, that’d be civil engineering or design architecture. It’s there in medical imaging. It’s in getting us to the Moon. The lunar module was controlled by a tiny computer that only had to think about 20 or 30 variables at a time to steer the LEM down to the Moon’s surface. But it’s not something a person could do in their head.

Big Math is not simply big in the ordinary sense of big numbers. There’s an undercurrent in the book about the importance of linearity and sparsity. When problems are linear, they can be scaled up to immense size, and they stay tractable. If they’re non-linear, you don’t have any hope of dealing with them with pure math. You can do them with computers. But for the people who want rigorous theorems, we’re in terra incognita when we deal with anything that’s non-linear and big.

Small math can be very hard. Historically we had small math problems with one or two variables, arithmetic, algebra that you learn in high school. Even to this day, a lot of pure math is relatively small. It doesn’t concern itself with enormous numbers of variables simultaneously because it’s totally intractable. Cryptography can be small, but it’s so complicated with its non-linearities and other algebraic difficulties. So big is one way to be difficult, but there are a lot of other ways.

Nowadays, trillions of parameters are typical in the biggest of the large language models. But infinity is not big from this point. Infinity, which you would think of would be the biggest of big math, is not because calculus fits perfectly nicely in a human head. Infinity turns out to be very tractable in a way that a billion is not.

Ars Technica: One of your real-world examples of big math is particularly relevant at the moment: mathematics as it relates to the gerrymandering issue.

Steven Strogatz: It’s an interesting question of, what’s a fair map and what’s a reasonable redistricting? The legal system gives us certain criteria, but they are vague. The districts should not be too snakey-looking, not look like salamanders, for instance. The term in the law is ‘compact.’ They should be reasonably compact. But what does that mean mathematically? The districts are also supposed to be contiguous; you don’t want to have separate islands and call that one district. It’s supposed to respect natural boundaries like rivers or other landscape features. Also, each district is supposed to have roughly the same population.

So how to make maps that respect all of the criteria? It turns out there are a lot of ways to make them. Using past voting information, you can stack the deck. That’s what gerrymandering is all about, trying to redistrict in favor of whatever party is in power. There has been a big debate about whether certain maps violate people’s rights. The principle should be one person, one vote. If you do things that dilute somebody’s power so that their votes are wasted, so to speak, that’s traditionally thought to be unfair or maybe even illegal.

The Supreme Court has heard all kinds of cases, but the court is very resistant to the use of math. I quoted [Chief Justice] John Roberts talking about the efficiency gap as one measure of gerrymandering. He referred to it by its Greek letters and said something like, “The average person on the street is just going to think this is gobbledygook, that this is not a criterion that the law should pay any attention to.”

Nevertheless, Moon Duchin and her colleagues showed this very down-to-earth way of generating a big ensemble of reasonable maps that satisfy all the legal criteria so that you have a baseline, you could do the control experiment. What the math does is give you a principled way of generating maps at random that respect the criteria that the law requires, but otherwise are just a random sample of reasonable maps. How often would a map produce the result that the gerrymander intended? If you can show that it’s an  extreme outlier, that’s a measure of something. Does it prove malicious intent? Not really. It does prove it’s not typical.

There was an Alabama voting rights case where Roberts actually voted with the plaintiffs, who said that there was real harm being done to Black voters in Alabama with one of Alabama’s maps. The state was claiming there’s no way we could get a reasonable map to have two districts with Black majority voters. Moon Duchin showed that was wrong. You could generate thousands of maps that would do that. So it just wasn’t true what the state had claimed.

After the book was copyedited and ready to go to press, a Supreme Court decision was made that in fact you can partisan gerrymander as much as you want. It doesn’t violate the Voting Rights Act. We wrote a more optimistic story.

Ars Technica: How has math changed over time from a philosophical standpoint?

Steven Strogatz: I think a crisp way to encapsulate it is the role of the computer. For a long time, a computer was a servant to mathematicians and engineers and scientists. It was no more philosophically threatening than a microscope or a telescope. It could extend our senses. It’s a chisel, it’s an anvil, it’s a tool. It helped us do calculations faster; things that would be too hard for people to do at scale you could do on a machine. We had mechanical calculators. They became electronic computers in the 1950s with the invention of the transistor and got much faster to give us today’s supercomputers. We program them, they do exactly what we tell them. So they’re servants.

The first philosophically big change is in the 1950s, when computers start to be used as more than servants; they become scouts. You could send the computer out into unknown, unexplored territory in the mathematical landscape. The clearest example was work by Enrico Fermi and his colleagues (Pasta, Ulam, and Tsingou) that we wrote about in the chapter on simulation. Mary Tsingou was the one who did the calculations in what was traditionally called the Fermi-Pasta-Ulam numerical experiment.

What distinguished it from earlier uses of computers is that Fermi and colleagues had no idea what the answer was going to be. It’s one thing to use a computer to calculate the power released by an atomic bomb blast or to calculate an artillery shell’s trajectory. We didn’t know the exact numerical values, but we knew what was happening. With this experiment, they were trying to simulate a simplified model of how atoms could, in the jargon, formalize as chemical bonds jiggle. You want to see the decay to entropy. How do systems degenerate to greater and greater disorder if they’re just left to their own devices?

Fermi thought that the non-linear aspect of chemical bonds—meaning if you’d stretch a chemical bond by twice as much, it doesn’t pull back exactly twice as hard, so it’s not strictly proportional—might account for the so-called second law of thermodynamics, the decay to entropy. Pencil and paper math was not up to the job because you couldn’t calculate these non-linear interactions far enough to see the decay into entropy.

So they ran it in the computer, and a shocking thing happened: It didn’t decay. It showed a strange echo. You started all the energy in one of the modes of vibration of this system, and then it started to degenerate. But after a while, 98 percent of the energy came back into the original mode. That was the echo that they called a recurrence phenomenon. It led us to a whole new branch of math that we call soliton theory. It was an early indication that once you walk into the nonlinear jungle, human intuition breaks down. We couldn’t walk there, but we sent our scout, this computer probe, into the wilderness of the mind, and it was the only thing capable of doing the calculations and reporting back to us what it found.

Ars Technica: The rapid rise of AI in mathematics has alarmed many in the field, particularly in light of OpenAI’s release last month of 722 AI-generated math papers, dubbed the “mathocalypse.” Technically, AI is also a new tool, but it seems fundamentally different.

Steven Strogatz: So from servant to scout was a new thing, and computing was our partner. At first, it was all very thrilling. It was like having a new Ferrari or something. You could race ahead and then discover things. That’s what Fermi and Ulam said at the time. That was truly amazing. They didn’t know what to expect. The computer was now teaching us by showing us things, but we could still keep up. We could develop these new theories like soliton theory, so we could rationalize what it was showing us.

What’s different now is that it’s going from servant to scout to oracle, where it’s showing us things that we don’t understand and it’s not articulate enough to explain itself. It seems to be right. A clear example would be something like Google Translate, where it’s fantastic at translating one language into another, but it has not discovered new principles of linguistics, or if it has, it can’t tell us what they are.

Likewise, protein folding with AlphaFold that won the Nobel Prize in chemistry a couple of years ago for the people who built it. We now can solve a thousand times more proteins than we had before, but we have not done it by learning new physics or chemistry. It just recognized patterns, but it can’t tell us how it’s doing it. So that’s where we are, and it’s going to get worse.

Ars Technica: In your book, you ask if insight still matters, citing Stephen Wolfram as an example of someone who might answer that question in the negative.

Steven Strogatz: Wolfram’s position is tricky. He pushes computational irreducibility. I love these two words, perspicuous and perspicacious. Systems can be perspicuous, you can see into them. Science has for thousands of years concerned itself with systems that are perspicuous, meaning you can get insight into them because you’re so perspicacious. But the question from Wolfram would be, have we been the famous drunk that’s looking under the lamppost for his keys because it’s dark everywhere else? You wouldn’t have any hope of finding them except under the lamppost.

So is it that we’ve artificially restricted ourself to systems that are transparent enough that we can develop science that our insight illuminates? I think Wolfram would claim that most of nature is computationally irreducible in the sense that there is no simple insightful theory into what’s going on. The best you can do is just run a simulation and watch what happens, similar to just letting nature unfold and watch what happens in reality.

In the jargon people use nowadays, you shouldn’t expect a compressed description, a simplified model that will account for what you’re seeing. We have seen those. Newton could explain the motion of the planets from a few simple principles and that worked, but that’s, I think in Wolfram’s take, artificially simple. Most of science is not going to end up being like that. We don’t know if Wolfram is really right about that. Wolfram, I think, is demonstrably right that many computer simulations are computationally irreducible, but nature might not be.

Ars Technica: If you can’t see into the models, and they can’t explain what they’re doing, how do you know they’re right?

Steven Strogatz: Let’s stipulate that we don’t understand them, and that they are not at the present able to explain themselves to us. Let’s put a little asterisk next to that part, though, because it could be that they’ll become very good teachers. Or maybe we don’t understand Google Translate or AlphaFold right now, but we will in 10 years. I do believe that there will be systems that are truly opaque because of human limits, that we just won’t be fast enough or smart enough or have enough memory. AIs will show us something that we just won’t be able to understand even with their help. What happens then?

Then I think math starts to be more like science or medicine, or engineering. At the FDA, for a new drug to be approved, it has to be shown to be safe by doing a lot of controlled studies and randomized trials. There’s a whole methodology for opacity that people in medicine have worked out. But the doctors do not require knowledge of the mechanism of action. You don’t have to know how aspirin works. You don’t have to know how electroconvulsive therapy works for people with intractable depression. Even if we don’t understand, we can still live with these technologies or drugs by these methods. You don’t have to have first principles understanding. If the AI is an oracle, you can still check it by seeing how often it is right.

Ars Technica: What are we losing in the process?

Steven Strogatz: It is a loss. I don’t want to pooh-pooh the severity of the—to use a fancy philosophical word—epistemic loss. What we have prided ourselves on in pure math for so long is that we’re the only subject that has absolute proof. We have certainty. We’re as close to certainty as anything that humanity can do. We can write a proof. Euclid’s proofs are still correct today, even though he wrote them in 300 BC. There’s nothing in the science or medicine of 300 BC that is still accepted, but all of the math is still true.

So there is something eternal and very special about math. Even though we’re just primates, somehow we’re tapping into something really deep. And so to say, “Well, science and engineering are the models for math,” would be a big sacrifice to the purest of pure mathematicians. They don’t want to give up the certainty that we’ve always been so proud of.

Ars Technica: There’s a sadness among many mathematicians, not just for the epistemic questions, but also for the impact on why we do math in the first place. You write about that “aha!” moment when you’re struggling with a problem and suddenly you see the pattern. Are we losing that?

Steven Strogatz: There’s so many things that mathematicians are in grief about, those of us who feel grief. I have been through the whole sequence of the five stages of grief. I’m joking with you a little. But the young students don’t think this is funny. I’m an old person at the end of my career, so it’s one thing for me to be philosophical about it. But imagine being 25 years old and you love this subject and you’ve dreamt about being a mathematician, and now people are saying the subject is over. There are even people gloating about it. Some of the people in the tech community are very cruel about what’s happening to math.

We talked about the epistemic aspect, but there’s also the culture of productive struggle. The pure math people like Terry Tao have been emphasizing understanding. That’s our idea of insight, that when you try hard to understand something, even if you don’t solve it, you often are so resourceful in thinking of strategies or other simplified versions of the problem. You learn a lot on the journey. You also have all this companionship and human joy of being with other people who love the thing you love. This is not restricted to math. The people who love art or weaving—my colleagues in creative writing are bereft over what their students are experiencing now, handing in AI [generated work]. They want writing with soul, with human individuality.

Ars Technica: That is the opposite of what AI does. I guess I worry that it will do the same thing to math. There is a beauty to math, an aesthetic element. If all you’re focused on is getting the answers and ignore the journey, those are qualitative losses. Mathematicians have individual voices, too.

Steven Strogatz: Totally. There are people whose style is very visible in the questions they ask, in the approach they take to them, in the way they explain themselves. I have cried on camera for several interviews over the past few months because there are lots of things maybe being lost. But I want to put an underline on the fact that we don’t really know. For example, what if AI gets really good at teaching us? I find when I interact with it, that it can be a very effective explainer.

And why do we have to feel sad? Maybe we can understand math better than ever with the help of this machine. We may not get to make the greatest discoveries because the machine will probably be making them, but we can still make little discoveries. I never made a great discovery. I’m not at the level of a Fields medalist, but I can still enjoy math and so can people way below me. There are recreational mathematicians who take pleasure in discovery, whatever they figure out on their own. Just like when a person solves a puzzle, it’s not like a jigsaw puzzle or Sudoku is important. You just have the pleasure of thinking.

There are people who love the workout part of it. There are people who like the competition. We shouldn’t pretend. There are people who are thrilled by the idea of being the first to make a big discovery. That’s animating for some folks and it will be hard for them.

Ars Technica: Is there any reason for hope?

Steven Strogatz: So far the AIs are not good at asking questions. I think they could be trained to ask good questions. A graduate student is often not good at asking questions when they start because in college you’re not taught to ask questions, you’re taught to give answers. The hardest thing in training a new PhD or postdoc is helping them figure out how to ask questions. I think we could do that with AIs, too.

There is a feeling of loss, but I don’t think we should shortchange the feeling of excitement. There’s so many practical, important benefits that could happen in energy technology and climate and medicine, theoretical physics, pure math. I mean, we’re making astonishing discoveries through this machine. So for a lot of people, it’s a very joyful time. It’s very confusing. That’s the only honest assessment.

We end the book with Faust. Usually people hear “Faustian bargain” as, “You have sold your soul to the devil, now you’re going to pay.” Goethe’s version is more ambiguous: that if we keep striving to build something bigger than ourselves, maybe we can be redeemed. But it’s still a very dangerous game that we have chosen to play.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

The Outer Worlds 2 PS5 DLC Split into Two Parts, First in December

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Obsidian still wants to carry out its post-launch DLC plans for The Outer Worlds 2, having lost 25% of its workforce in a recent round of layoffs. It's today announced an expansion called Side Effect Guaranteed will release in two parts: the first launches for PS5 on 2nd December 2026, and then the second arrives in Spring 2027. See it in action in the trailer.

The new DLC takes place just before the end of the main game, so if you've already finished a playthrough, there'll be a new option on the starting menu screen that lets you access the content through a new character.

A distress signal beckons you and your crew of the Incognito to a derelict ship, where you discover the ship’s crew in suspended animation. All signs point to foul play, especially the puddle of what was most likely a person that you recover a video message from. It was left by the captain of the vessel, who explains that the crew has been contaminated with something while on a heist that will cause explosive cell death unless they find a cure. And by explosive cell death, she means more like a… “liquefaction”.

Side Effect Guaranteed adds a new region to explore called Elysium Valley, another recruitable character, a level cap increase to 36, more than 15 new perks, four extra flaws, and additional gear (weapons, armour, and outfits). You can also look forward to an additional 20 songs.

The expansion costs $14.99, or if you bought the Premium Edition of the game, you'll gain instant access to the DLC.

Review The Outer Worlds 2 (PS5) - Mechanically Fantastic RPG Struggles to Be Memorable

It'll work to expand and improve an RPG we thought was pretty good at release, awarding it a 7/10 rating. "The Outer Worlds 2 succeeds in being a bigger and better sequel, buoyed by an even greater emphasis on player choice and freedom. Its RPG mechanics are largely fantastic, and there are key improvements to both combat and exploration," we said at the time. "However, despite Obsidian's clever writing, there's an underlying dreariness to the property that it just can't seem to escape."

Opinion: Sign him, Liverpool: South American star is screaming 'come and get me'

One South American Premier League star is just begging for Liverpool to sign him. It's time to get a deal done.

Liverpool have a selection of superstars in their squad right now. That's the result of an unprecedented spend over the last couple of years, with the Reds repeatedly breaking their transfer record.

It was viewed as the way to go post-Jurgen Klopp. They wanted to sign as much talent as possible, taking advantage of the position that Klopp left them in, essentially.

The result? It's not a great squad. The talent pool is fantastic, no question there, but Liverpool have been unable to piece things together to create a bigger picture.

It's why we're not sure that Liverpool's next big moves will be for players who glue things together, rather than lead the side. And that simply must point them strongly in one direction.

It's Diego Gomez againWhat do Liverpool need? Well, they need more energy on and off the ball. They need a right-winger. They need a midfielder. They need more space in attack. They could really use more defensive work. Congratulations! You (by which I mean I) have described Diego Gomez. The Brighton star is essentially everything Liverpool need right now. He's a right-sided midfielder who can also play through the middle. Not just as an attacking midfielder, either, but as a covering defensive midfielder, or a box-to-box. What do Liverpool need? Well, they need more energy on and off the ball. They need a right-winger. They need a midfielder. They need more space in attack. They could really use more defensive work.

Congratulations! You (by which I mean I) have described Diego Gomez. The Brighton star is essentially everything Liverpool need right now.

He's a right-sided midfielder who can also play through the middle. Not just as an attacking midfielder, either, but as a covering defensive midfielder, or a box-to-box.

The Paraguayan also isn't a superstar who requires lots of the ball. Gomez is happy to take a backseat to others and play a supporting role - and an incredibly strong one.

There is a reason, after all, why Brighton rest Gomez in the UEFA Conference League. He's played all but 23 minutes in the Premier League for the side currently in second and is the kind of player that elite teams thrive with - someone who wins the ball back, makes lots of runs, and pops up with incredible moments.

We've seen two of those this season. The first was a ridiculous goal from halfway. The second was a sensational goal against Brentford this weekend that was essentially a "come and get me plea" to any top clubs looking for that 'glue' player.

We wrote back in August that Gomez is the kind of player Liverpool need. Our Value Adder model is one that pushes him as as an incredibly valuable footballer who's still only 23 and flying under the radar.

Now, he's no longer flying particularly far under that radar. People are picking him up thanks to these wonder goals. But with Brighton almost certainly facing massive offers for their bigger 'stars' in 2027, we think there's a chance to tempt them with an offer here.

Gomez is what Liverpool need. He's worth a fortune to a club like the Reds who are just a player or two away from having a very balanced team.

Serie A: AS Roma crushes Sassuolo and returns to one point of 1st place

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

AS Roma did not tremble! Opposed to Sassuolo this Saturday during the 11th day of Serie A, Gian Piero Gasperini’s men largely won at home (3-0) and achieved an excellent performance in the standings. Third before the match, the Romans had the opportunity to come back one step behind their historic rival, Lazio, leader of the championship. And they didn’t miss their chance. Dominant from the first minutes, the Giallorossi found the opening in the 27th minute thanks to Mario Hermoso. After a one-two with Donyell Malen and a mix-up in the opposing defense, the Spanish defender recovered the ball at the edge of the area before unleashing a powerful strike in the middle, deceiving Arijanet Muric (1-0). Three minutes later, Roma broke with some success. On a cross-shot from Malen, Sebastian Walukiewicz accidentally deflected the ball into his own net (30th, CSC, 2-0). A comfortable advantage that Gasperini’s players maintained until the break, controlling the debates and monopolizing the ball.

Returning from the locker room, the Romans continued their domination and did not take long to definitely make the difference. In the 57th minute, Rodrigo Mora completed a superb collective action initiated on the left side. After combining with Malen in a perfectly executed one-two, the young Portuguese attacking midfielder adjusted Muric with a cross shot to score his team’s third goal (3-0). A clear victory for Roma who completely stifled their opponent, with 27 shots attempted throughout the match and only one shot on target conceded. Established for the second consecutive time due to Mile Svilar’s injury, Colombian goalkeeper Devis Vásquez was hardly ever called upon. Thanks to this success, AS Roma temporarily climbs to second place in Serie A and ensures that it will spend the international break on the podium. For its part, Sassuolo falls to 17th place, just above the red zone, and could fall among the relegated if Monza wins against Atalanta. After the break, the Romans will travel to Parma, while Sassuolo will host Genoa.

A little confused as to how the whole thing goes down? Here’s what to expect tonight.

As the show puts it in their official press release: “Two at a time, couples face off against one another on the ballroom floor as they dance head-to-head in the same style at the same time. Winners of each of the three Dance-Offs will be selected by the judges, and the winning couple from each dance-off will receive bonus points added to their judges’ totals for the night.”

Here’s an example from a few seasons back:

Here are the pairings, the songs, and the dance styles:

Amber and Pasha vs. Ezra and DaniBoth couples will perform a rumba to “The Boy Is Mine,” performed live by Brandy and Monica.

Connor and Rylee vs. Maura and MarkBoth couples will perform a cha-cha to “All I Wanna Do,” performed live by Sheryl Crow.

These two have immunity thanks to getting the highest scores last week! Which means judges will automatically add the dance-off bonus points to their score this week.

Jimmy Carter’s Family Farm Just Sold for $8.5 Million

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The Georgia property will become part of a nearly 7,000-acre wildlife management area, with public access possible as early as 2027.

President Jimmy Carter’s family farmland near Plains, Georgia, was just sold to the Conservation Fund as part of an approximately $8.5 million acquisition that will protect nearly 2,500 acres of fields and woodland. Announced November 9, the deal sets the stage for the land’s eventual transfer to the state of Georgia, with public access potentially beginning as early as 2027.

The purchase encompasses approximately 1,600 acres owned by the Carter family, along with an adjoining tract. It does not include Carter’s boyhood home and farm, which are already preserved by the National Park Service as part of Jimmy Carter National Historical Park.

Instead, the newly acquired property is slated to join the nearby Lanahassee Creek Wildlife Management Area, expanding it to nearly 7,000 acres. The land provides habitat for gopher tortoises and other wildlife, while neighboring creeks support rare freshwater mussels. Plans call for the Georgia Department of Natural Resources to take ownership in 2028.

The acquisition also continues restoration work Carter initiated himself. At his request, the family preserved a stand of mature longleaf pine and replanted much of the surrounding acreage with the same native species. Once used to grow crops, including peanuts, the property became a retreat where he and Rosalynn spent time away from public life. The family acquired the land around the turn of the 20th century, according to the Conservation Fund.

“They liked to go and walk the land,” Blake Sullivan, who managed Carter’s farm and timberland for more than four decades, told The Washington Post. “For both of them, it was a place of rest and renewal.”

Carter continued visiting into his final years, arranging to be driven through the property when walking became difficult. Amy Carter, the president’s daughter, told The Post, “I think his very favorite place was to be in the town and on the land where he grew up, and where he had those very deep roots.”

Find a gift that kids will actually like

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

When it comes to gifting, kids can be particular. The things kids like change not just with age, but also with evolving trends from TV shows and new technology. If parents are lucky enough, their kid will write a long list of the precise items they'd like as a birthday or Christmas or Hannukkah gift and put it up on the fridge with pride. But sometimes, it can be as much of a crapshoot as coming up with a gift idea for someone else's kid. Without further ado, here are the best gifts for kids of all ages — including toy and non-toy gifts alike.

Everyone is familiar with the "terrible twos" rep that the not-a-baby-but-not-yet-an-independent-kid stage of development gets. At this age, kids are bouncing constantly between the desire to explore on their own and the reliance on parents for most basic needs like eating, getting dressed, or going to the bathroom. That's a lot of frustrating emotions to go through in a day. Kitchen play sets, toys that involve different motor skills, and anything with music are gifts that can let toddlers feel in charge without too much assistance from a parent — making them ideal for this age category.

Potentially a more daunting phase than the terrible twos: The one where the pressure to be cool is relentless. This is that age. Though "cool" can lend itself to multiple interpretations, gifts that contribute to an older kid's evolving critical thinking skills and sense of independence, responsibility, and style are all smart ideas. These could be anything from STEM toys to beginner's makeup tools to a fitness tracker or a pretend pet that encourages routine-making. Anything that involves an app, tablet, or e-reader is also a guaranteed win.

Finding a gift that a teenager will think is cool (and that will be on-trend for longer than a few months) might be some of the trickiest shopping you'll do all year. They'll probably offer up a few big softball suggestions, but if you're going the surprise route, remember that they care about brands. Thanks to social media influencers promoting products directly to teens on Instagram or TikTok, Gen Z kids are loyal to very specific brands of tech, accessories, and even water bottles. If you follow the trends as closely as they do (trust us, we do), cracking the "best gifts for teenagers" code isn't rocket science.

Norway’s Electric Snowplow Worked. Sweden Added Hydrogen Anyway.

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Norway has already demonstrated battery-electric snowplowing on one of its toughest mountain highways. A Scania 45 R covered roughly 7,000 km on Hardangervidda, plowing, grading and spreading sand through a Norwegian winter. Its performance exceeded expectations. Meanwhile, Sweden has unveiled a hydrogen snowplow that is, in fact, another large battery-electric truck, with a fuel cell and hydrogen tanks added for extra range. The engineering question is why that second energy system is worth having when batteries are already doing the work, and when a conventional generator running on renewable diesel could offer a simpler, potentially cheaper route to occasional extended operation.

The Swedish vehicle has to compete with more than diesel snowplows. My full TFIE Strategy Briefing examines Norway’s measured electric operation, China’s expanding snow-removal equipment market and the additional costs hydrogen must justify.

Sweden’s project brings together Scania, MaserFrakt and Peab. The vehicle carries a 356 kWh rechargeable battery and 56 kg of hydrogen. The fuel cell generates electricity to extend the truck’s operating time between charging sessions. That gives the vehicle two energy-storage systems, plus the equipment to convert hydrogen into electricity. It will still need electric charging, and it will also need hydrogen production, delivery, storage and refueling.

Norway’s experience makes the starting point uncomfortable for hydrogen advocates. The Norwegian Public Roads Administration’s 2025 trial demonstrated that a production-based heavy electric truck could do demanding winter-maintenance work. Its measured consumption averaged approximately 2.02 kWh/km, with around six and a half hours of operating endurance under evaluated mixed-duty conditions. The trial identified shortcomings in charging compatibility and payload, but these were engineering and operating problems to resolve, not evidence that battery-electric snowplows were incapable of doing the job.

China is moving toward broader deployment. Municipalities have used smaller electric snow-removal equipment for years, while manufacturers have developed full-sized specialized electric vehicles. Beijing’s Mentougou district awarded contracts for 24 multifunction snow-removal trucks in September 2026. The final chassis specifications have not all been confirmed as battery-electric, but the procurement demonstrates a market moving beyond isolated demonstrations.

China’s much larger battery-electric truck industry is also providing specialized equipment manufacturers with an expanding supply of powertrains, batteries and heavy-duty chassis. Electric snowplows benefit from components developed and manufactured for freight vehicles at much greater volumes.

The Swedish approach looks increasingly difficult to justify against that background. If six or seven hours of productive operation is adequate, a battery-electric truck can already do the work. If longer shifts are required, larger batteries, better charging arrangements, mobile charging equipment or operational changes deserve assessment first. Hydrogen cannot claim the extreme-duty market merely because charging requires planning.

There is another alternative that the hydrogen comparison tends to omit. A conventional generator running on hydrotreated vegetable oil, or HVO, could supply additional electricity when the battery needs support. Diesel generators, fuel tanks and liquid-fuel distribution systems are mature technologies. Major generator manufacturers already approve suitable equipment for HVO, which can use conventional diesel-type storage and handling systems. Integrating a generator into a particular electric truck still requires engineering, certification and emissions controls, but it avoids constructing a hydrogen supply chain for a small fleet of seasonal vehicles.

For some operators, the even easier solution is to retain existing diesel snow-removal equipment and use certified renewable diesel for the relatively few extreme shifts that electric vehicles cannot cover. Copenhagen Airport has already adopted that approach, progressively electrifying its equipment while using HVO in compatible heavy vehicles, including snow-removal machines.

HVO is not unlimited, and its climate benefits depend on feedstock origin and production. Burning it also produces exhaust emissions. These constraints are important, particularly in populated areas. But a relatively small quantity used for exceptional winter duty is a different proposition from trying to replace a large share of road transportation with biofuels.

The hydrogen proposal must beat all these alternatives. It needs to demonstrate that the additional hours gained from the fuel cell are worth the vehicle cost, maintenance, fuel premium and refueling infrastructure. It also needs to outperform the cost of providing those hours with conventional generating equipment or retaining a small reserve fleet.

Snow-removal authorities have an obligation to maintain roads during severe weather. They should purchase equipment that meets that obligation with the lowest defensible whole-system cost and emissions. Norway has shown that batteries can perform demanding winter work, and China’s industrial scale is making specialized electric vehicles more accessible. Sweden’s hydrogen truck is entering a market in which the simpler electric solution is already working.

The full TFIE Strategy Briefing analysis examines the operating results, procurement evidence, charging limitations, airport fuel-cell trials and the practical alternatives that make hydrogen’s business case so difficult.

Scientists figured out how to use a cheap everyday camera to measure how well solar panels work

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

New digital tools turn massive amounts of complex data into simple, actionable insights to help leaders make better conservation decisions.

Solar panels glow if you run electricity into them in the dark. While photos easily show the bright and dim patches, measuring the exact brightness used to require an expensive, manually calibrated industrial camera.

Jürgen Werner and her research team from the University of Stuttgart, the Research Center Jülich, and the German company Solarzentrum Stuttgart got the same kind of number out of a $450 consumer camera, after writing down how its own electronics respond to light.

The camera is the easy part. A model of how it converts light into numbers is what makes the reading possible, and that model still leans on one reference cell with a known quality.

Push current into a solar cell and it behaves like an LED, giving off light of its own. Silicon cells glow in the infrared range, which the eye can’t see, so the picture has to come from a camera that can.

Engineers already use those pictures to check solar panels for cracked cells and broken connections. What they read is contrast. Bright areas are doing their job, dark ones aren’t.

The brightness itself is a measurement. The fraction of electrons that leave a solar cell as light instead of heat is its luminescent quantum efficiency.

The cell’s open-circuit voltage – the voltage it holds when nothing draws power from it – determines that fraction. Every tenfold rise adds about 60 mV to that voltage.

“An electroluminescence image contains much more quantitative information than simply showing bright and dark regions,” said Werner, of the Institute for Photovoltaics at the University of Stuttgart.

“With a suitable physical camera model and calibration, it can provide absolute luminescent quantum efficiency and, therefore, information about the local quality of a solar cell or module.”

Werner worked with Georgette Udo at Forschungszentrum Jülich and Liviu Stoicescu at Solarzentrum Stuttgart, a German company that inspects photovoltaic systems. Their camera was a Canon EOS 4000D, a low-cost model selling for around €400 in 2026.

Most consumer cameras carry a film over the sensor that absorbs infrared light, so the team took that film out. Astronomy hobbyists make the same change to shoot the night sky.

Then they screwed a long-pass filter onto the lens, which blocks the visible light that would otherwise wash out the faint glow.

The researchers took every photograph in the dark. A current source pushed a known current through each module, and the shutter time, the aperture and the ISO setting decided how much glow reached the sensor.

Brightness was then averaged across a whole cell in ImageJ, free image software, by drawing a box over the cell by hand.

Here is the problem that the model had to solve. A camera doesn’t report light honestly all the way up.

Below a certain brightness the number a pixel returns rises in step with the light that hits it, but above it the pixel fills up and the response stops rising.

That is over-exposure, familiar from any photograph taken into the sun.

Werner’s team measured where their own camera crossed over. It stayed linear below a brightness value of 400, and above that it bent toward a ceiling, which for one module sat at 1,177.

Both stretches are usable once the shape of the curve is known, and the saturated one yields a second estimate of the same quantity.

Converting brightness into quantum efficiency needs an anchor. The team took theirs from a cell with an established efficiency – 1.6 percent at 738 mV – and scaled everything else against it.

Eight silicon modules went under the camera, picked to span decades of production. The oldest was a Solarex panel built in 1978, with a module efficiency of 5.7 percent. At the other end, a Trina module from 2022 reached 21.1 percent.

A modern cell turns far more of its current into light, and by that measure a better cell is simply a brighter one.

Cells in the old panel averaged 560 mV of open-circuit voltage and a luminescent quantum efficiency of 0.0007 percent. Cells in the newer module ran at 687 mV and 0.23 percent.

Across those 45 years the quantum efficiency climbed by roughly a factor of 300.

The model had to fit both ends of that span of solar power manufacturing, and it mostly did. Readings landed where the 60 mV rule predicts, apart from four modules built with multicrystalline solar cells, which trap light less well than single-crystal ones.

Those four only fell into line after the team accounted for the difference.

Werner’s team didn’t put the camera up against an industrial one. The claim is narrower. A model of how a camera turns photons into numbers lets an ordinary one produce absolute values.

The weak points are the ones the authors name themselves. A box drawn by hand over each cell picks up the dark gaps between them, which put scatter in the data. An automatic version still has to be written.

Every measurement was made in the dark, on silicon, on modules whose performance was already known. And the whole scale depends on that single reference cell.

So far the authors haven’t pointed the calibrated camera at a module nobody has measured. That test would show whether the method grades hardware or only confirms what was known.

“Our next step is to use the calibrated camera to determine quantum efficiencies and open-circuit voltages of further, previously uncharacterized solar cells and modules,” Werner said.

He added that the same model should work on a panel lit up rather than powered up, and possibly outdoors.

Daylight matters most. The method needs a dark room, which keeps it in a laboratory. Outdoors, the camera could be carried along a row of panels at a working solar farm.

The full study was published in The Journal of Applied Physics.

Kimber Digital Securities gains broker-dealer status for tokenized assets

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The Plume affiliate plans to offer tokenized US stocks, ETFs and bonds through self-custodial wallets, with traditional shareholder rights preserved.

Kimber Digital Securities, an affiliate of blockchain network Plume, said Tuesday it had registered as a broker-dealer with the US Securities and Exchange Commission and joined the Financial Industry Regulatory Authority.

In its announcement, the company said it plans to offer tokenized US stocks, exchange-traded funds (ETFs) and bonds that investors could hold in self-custodial wallets. The company gave no launch date.

Tokenized stocks are digital tokens that represent company shares or rights tied to those shares. Transfers of the tokens are recorded on a blockchain. The rights investors receive depend on how the tokens are structured.

Unlike wrapped securities, the proposed tokens would represent existing ownership rights recorded through the Depository Trust Company (DTC). The company said investors would retain their dividend and voting rights and could transfer eligible holdings between traditional brokerage accounts and onchain wallets.

The company also plans to offer yield-bearing investment products to stablecoin users, including tokenized shares in funds managed by major US asset managers.

Kimber’s registration comes as other companies prepare to launch around-the-clock trading of tokenized US securities. On Monday, Formation Securities announced plans for a Solana-based trading venue offering tokenized shares of Galaxy Digital, Exodus Movement and Forward Industries, with a launch targeted for early 2027.

Major US exchange operators are pursuing similar initiatives ahead of 2027. The New York Stock Exchange is developing a 24/7 tokenized securities platform using Avalanche for settlement, while Nasdaq is targeting the second quarter of 2027 for its tokenized equity initiative with Kraken’s parent, Payward.

The value of tokenized stocks onchain has surged more than sixfold over the past year, rising to $3.87 billion from $643.3 million in November 2025.

Ring’s AI-Powered Drone Automatically Patrols Inside Your Home

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Ring’s AI-Powered Drone Automatically Patrols Inside Your Home

Home security can now fly. Amazon-owned Ring has announced the Ring Drone Cam, a $250 indoor flying security camera designed to automatically patrol a home’s interior.

The AI-powered Ring Drone Cam works alongside existing Ring products, including static cameras and sensors. When an alarm sounds or motion is detected, the drone automatically deploys to investigate.

Homeowners can also launch the drone on command or program custom flight routines on a scheduled basis. Due to its compact, lightweight design, flight times are limited to eight minutes per charge. Users can monitor a live 360-degree feed on their smartphones while the drone is airborne, and built-in LED spotlights allow it to operate effectively in total darkness.

Although designed to operate autonomously, initial setup requires some manual effort: users must carry the drone throughout their home to help it build an internal spatial map. Additionally, Ring notes that navigation works best in environments with distinct visual details; reflective surfaces, featureless walls, and frameless glass may pose operational challenges.

Detailed camera specifications remain sparse, but Ring confirms the drone features a 2K (1920×1920) square image sensor paired with a 148-degree field of view.

After completing a patrol, the drone automatically returns to its base station, fully recharging in roughly an hour. Ring emphasizes that all video and audio recording automatically stops whenever the drone is docked.

With a subscription, users can store, share, and review recorded footage—including motion events and Live View feeds—for up to 180 days. Users can also search through archival footage using plain-text queries.

Designed exclusively for indoor use, the premise behind the Ring Drone Cam is simple: a single autonomous drone can monitor the same space as multiple stationary security cameras.

Ring founder Jamie Siminoff told The Verge during a launch event that the company has spent a decade developing the drone off and on, only recently achieving its design and performance goals after returning to the drawing board a year ago.

Although Amazon’s listing notes the device has not yet received FCC authorization, Siminoff states that the U.S.-assembled drone complies with all regulatory standards.

The Ring Drone Cam is scheduled to ship in January 2027 for $249.99.

How to get the Sinking Lure in Zelda Ocarina of Time

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The Sinking Lure is an optional item you can find in The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake, but the conditions for finding it are a little confusing. The lure itself isn't necessary for beating the game, and you can't even leave the fishing pond with it, so don't fret if this is something you're stuck on.

Below, we explain where to find the Sinking Lure in the Ocarina of Time remake, as well as what you can use the lure on.

Being honest, the conditions for getting the Sinking Lure to show up are a little confusing. We've explained our experiences across three different save files below.

For one, you may need to win the big prize in at least one time period. As young Link, you'll need to catch a that is at least 50 cm long to win the Piece of Heart, or as adult Link, you'll need to catch a fish that is at least 60 cm long to win the Golden Scale. We had a save file that had the Golden Scale but no Piece of Heart, and were able to find the Sinking Lure as young Link. Similarly, we have a save file where we have the Piece of Heart, but no Golden Scale, and we were able to find the lure as adult Link.

Additionally, the lure will actually only show up after you pay the Fishing-Pond Man the rupees and rent the rod. In our tests, he says something about the lure (either being against the rules or that it keeps showing up in his pond), whenever the lure does appear.

There are a few places it can spawn: by the smaller waterfall pouring into a stone fountain on the north side of the lake, at the larger waterfall on the south side of the lake, or by debris inside the pond.

We also noticed that when testing things out for this guide, if you leave the area and then immediately come back, the Sinking Lure will not show up again until you fully leave the map and come back. For example, we ran in and out of the shop a few times to try to find the various spawn locations, but the Sinking Lure never showed up again until we warped to the Temple of Time and hoofed it back to the fishing pond. When we returned, we found the Sinking Lure again right away.

Well, as the Fishing-Pond Man warns you, if you use the Sinking Lure on any of the regular bass in the pond, he will not give you a reward, with one exception. If you manage to actually catch the Hylian loach at the bottom of the pond, he'll give you 50 rupees.

The Sinking Lure makes catching fish much easier, except for the loach. However, the Sinking Lure does make it a bit easier to hook the loach, because they spend a majority of their time underwater. The Hylian loach will require a lot of patience to catch, as it jumps out of the water and changes directions a lot, and it just takes longer to reel in. That being said, catching the Hylian loach is mainly for clout, as you don't earn anything outside the purple rupee for nabbing it.

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake is a little bit different than the original game, so if you need guides to help you through, we don’t blame you. We have walkthroughs for all the dungeons, from the Deku Tree to Ganondorf's Castle. We also have guides for the sillier, in-between parts of the game, like when you have to find Richard (the dog) or Cuccos.

For those going for 100% completion, we also have guides on where to find every Piece of Heart, every Gold Skulltula token, and where to plant every Magic Bean.

2027 Bugatti Tourbillon

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

See all results for new 2027 Bugatti Tourbillon for sale near 29405

In the world of fine chronometry, a "tourbillon" is a mechanical device within a watch's interior that counteracts the pull of gravity to ensure utmost accuracy. And while the Bugatti Tourbillon's artfully penned curves and handcrafted interior are just as dazzling to take in as the movement of a luxury timepiece, this four-wheeled wonder is much more than a $4.6 million piece of automotive jewelry.

A bespoke, naturally aspirated, 8.3-liter V-16 engine nestled into its carbon-fiber body structure behind the driver cements that fact, churning out 986 horsepower on its own. The addition of three electric motors—developed by parent company and electric sports car maker Rimac—ratchets output up to 1775 horsepower, making the Tourbillon Bugatti's most powerful model yet. Inside, the Tourbillon eschews large infotainment screens and extensive driver aids for expertly milled switchgear, top-shelf materials, and a more connected driving experience—a choice that its Audemars-Piguet-wearing clientele will surely appreciate.

Expert Tip: When it comes to seven-digit hypercars that prioritize luxury craftsmanship and outright speed in equal measure, the Tourbillon is in a class of its own. However, if you want to drop a couple of commas on a car that can go like hell on a racetrack, Ferrari's newest F80 flagship will do the trick.

The Tourbillon was revealed just last year. As a result, we don’t expect any notable changes to Bugatti's flagship for 2027.

If this year's model is largely unchanged and you're comfortable with a lightly used car, you may want to consider shopping previous model years. A one-to-three-year-old model will likely have a warranty and it'll cost less than buying new.

The Tourbillon is technically a hybrid, but the star of the show is a naturally aspirated 8.3-liter V-16 engine that's been designed with help from Cosworth and makes 986 horsepower and 664 pound-feet of torque. The big block is then paired with three electric motors, which boost combined output to an incredible 1775 ponies. The engine is backed by an eight-speed dual-clutch automatic transmission and all-wheel drive. We haven't had the chance to drive one yet, but we're obviously eager to do so. When we get the chance, we'll update this story with driving impressions and test results.

Car and Driver calculates your car's value in three free and easy steps - using the same Black Book® data dealers use to appraise vehicles.

Speaking of test results, here's one we can't wait to confirm: Bugatti says the Tourbillon can reach 60 mph in as little as 2.0 seconds and hit 250 mph in about 25 seconds. With all of that power, it might even be able to do the 60-mph dash even quicker in our testing.

In addition to the Tourbillon's three electric motors, this hypercar also carries a roughly 21.0-kWh battery pack that holds enough charge for about 30 miles of electric range.

EPA fuel economy estimates still aren't out yet for the Tourbillon. Even though it does boast a plug-in hybrid powertrain, the Tourbillon is not likely to be a high-mpg achiever. The Chiron, for reference, earned EPA ratings of 9 mpg city, 14 mpg highway, and 11 mpg combined. The Tourbillon may do slightly, but at the end of the day, it's a 1775-hp exotic, not a Prius. For more information about the Tourbillon's fuel economy, visit the EPA's website.

First Row: The interior of the Tourbillon is sculptural and mechanically fascinating, with a fixed-hub steering wheel that rotates around the gauges, so it never obscures the instrument cluster. In a collaboration befitting the Tourbillon's inspiration, a Swiss watchmaker builds the gauges, which feature raised numbers and physical needles. There's no plastic anywhere to be seen; even the clear coverings over the gauge face and center console are made of machined sapphire glass.

Cargo Capacity: While Bugatti says that the Tourbillon has more cargo space than the Chiron, that isn't saying much. The Chiron's trunk provided just two cubic feet of cargo space.

The Tourbillon was designed to be its own entertainment, but for those desperate to check texts or have a GPS connection, Bugatti has a small hide-away screen and Apple CarPlay connectivity.

The Tourbillon does not offer additional driver-assistance features, such as blind-spot monitoring. For more information about the Tourbillon's crash-test results, visit the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) and Insurance Institute for Highway Safety (IIHS) websites.

Bugatti offers a four-year warranty on all Tourbillon models and covers maintenance for the vehicle over the same time period.

Stephen Miller’s Wife Katie Miller Reportedly Ended Podcast Deal with Paramount After He Took White House Chief of Staff Role

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Katie Miller, a podcaster and the wife of new White House Chief of Staff Stephen Miller, as part of her husband’s recusals reportedly ended a planned podcast deal with Paramount, according to a report in The Atlantic.

Katie Miller on Sunday announced she would be ending new episodes of her existing podcast, “The Katie Miller Show,” to focus on her priorities as a mother to three children. The Atlantic also reported that Miller had ended a separate contract with Paramount over a planned podcast deal. Miller additionally ended her employment with Elon Musk’s SpaceX and sold her stock in the company as part of the recusals needed for her husband’s new role in the Trump White House. It’s unclear for which division of Paramount and parent company Skydance the deal was meant to be with. However, in May, Business Insider reported that Paramount was looking to add podcasts to Paramount+ as part of a larger content push to further compete with Netflix, YouTube, and other streamers. Business Insider at the time reported that Katie Miller’s podcast was among the ones with which Paramount was reportedly in talks.

Paramount did not return IndieWire’s request for comment. A White House Official told IndieWire, “Stephen Miller is no longer subject to any recusals and may fully participate in all meetings and policy matters.”

Miller’s podcast, sometimes referred to as the “Conservative Mom Podcast,” featured interviews with people such as Cheryl Hines, JD Vance, Nicki Minaj, and Tristan Thompson and focused on issues relevant to conservative women and mothers.

Stephen Miller, an architect of the Trump administration’s deportation policy, was promoted to Chief of Staff following the midterm elections, taking over from now-former Chief of Staff Susie Wiles.

Miller plans to still release two previously recorded episodes of her show.

2027 Lexus ES

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

See all results for new 2027 Lexus ES for sale near 98032

The ES350h epitomizes the quiet luxury experience that the Lexus brand is known for. Its standard 244-hp hybrid powertrain is fuel-efficient but not particularly exciting. The same can be said of the ES’s handling, which is tuned for comfort rather than for driving joy.

The cabin's environs are premium and offer similar interior space and amenities to rivals from Audi and BMW, but the ES starts at a lower price point than those two sporty sedans. The Lexus has credible competition from inside the Toyota ecosystem, though: the uniquely styled Crown sedan, which shares its platform and powertrain with the Lexus but starts at an even more attractive price.

Expert Tip: While Lexus stopped selling the V-6-powered ES when the car was redesigned for 2026, it did add an electric variant. We review the ES EV separately.

Compare the 2027 Lexus ES with the top vehicles in this segment with our new compare tool.

Lexus redesigned the ES for the 2026 model year, so the mid-size luxury sedan slides into 2027 without any changes.

If this year's model is largely unchanged and you're comfortable with a lightly used car, you may want to consider shopping previous model years. A one-to-three-year-old model will likely have a warranty and it'll cost less than buying new.

The ES is offered only in ES350h hybrid guise. Front-wheel-drive models are powered by a hybrid powertrain consisting of a 2.5-liter four-cylinder and two electric motors that combine for 244 horsepower. A continuously variable automatic transmission is standard. All-wheel drive models get a third electric motor that powers the rear axle, but combined horsepower remains the same at 244 horsepower.

The ES has always been a smooth operator, and this generation is no different. Lexus says this new model has more chassis rigidity than older models, and more attention has been spent on reducing body vibrations. When we drove the ES350h, we were impressed by its ride quality, but noted that it's no sports sedan, as its handling is still easy-going rather than sharp-edged.

Car and Driver calculates your car's value in three free and easy steps - using the same Black Book® data dealers use to appraise vehicles.

In Comparison: We haven't tested it yet, but the ES350h should be about as quick as the similar Toyota Crown, which needed 7.2 seconds to hit 60 mph at our test track.

The EPA rates the ES hybrid as high as 48 mpg city, 44 mpg highway, and 46 mpg combined—top-notch for a car of this size. When we get a chance, we'll take the ES on our 75-mph highway fuel economy route and update this story with real-world test results. For more information about the ES's fuel economy, visit the EPA's website.

First Row: With its stylish dash and high-quality cabin materials, the ES creates a plush environment for passengers. Ambient interior lighting and 3D-printed faux bamboo wood trim are used on the door panels for an upscale look. The seats are comfortable, and there's plenty of space for adults of all sizes to find their ideal driving position.

Second Row: A pair of passengers will also have no issues settling into the ES's comfortable back seat. Upscale environs await you in the rear compartment, with nicely cushioned seats and dedicated rear-seat air vents for the climate control.

Cargo Capacity: A large trunk gives the ES ample cargo space, and we expect to find it similarly sized to rivals. When we get a chance, we'll stuff the trunk with carry-on suitcases and report our findings here.

A 14.0-inch infotainment touchscreen takes a prominent location on the ES's dashboard. It features wireless connectivity for smartphones with Apple CarPlay and Android Auto, as well as native navigation. A 10-speaker stereo is standard, and a 17-speaker Mark Levinson premium stereo system is available as an option.

A host of driver-assistance features comes standard, including adaptive cruise control, blind-spot monitoring, and automatic high-beam headlamps.

For information about the ES's crash-test results, visit the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) and Insurance Institute for Highway Safety (IIHS) websites.

Lexus provides longer warranty coverage than BMW and Mercedes-Benz. However, it trails Genesis, which offers one of the best warranties in the segment.
