New in Civitas: "The Blue Tsunami Threatens To Drown Originalism"

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Mostly law professors | Sometimes contrarian | Often libertarian | Always independent

"Going forward, the path of advancing the conservative legal movement requires a political reorientation."

My new essay in Civitas tries to make sense of how the midterm election affects the broader conservative legal movement. Here is the introduction:

President Donald Trump's surprise 2016 election saved Justice Antonin Scalia's seat and perhaps the Supreme Court. Over the ensuing decade, Trump appointed scores of originalist judges at all levels of the judiciary and led a political movement that supported originalist state court judges and attorneys general. But that movement came to a screeching halt with the 2026 midterm election. Indeed, the blue tsunami, largely a referendum on Trump, threatens to drown originalism. In deep red states, conservative judges were washed off the bench. Also swept from office were conservative governors who appoint judges and attorneys general who litigate in those courts. Given the large Democratic Senate majority and unfavorable maps for 2028 and 2030, a Republican Senate with the votes to confirm originalist judges may not return until 2033 at the earliest. An entire generation of talent will be wasted. A necessary condition for a successful legal movement is a successful political party. Legal conservatives need to carefully reconsider the political circumstances best suited to defend the Constitution.

The conservative legal movement has always existed in conjunction and disjunction with the broader conservative political movement.  Certain long-term projects concerning the separation of powers and individual rights endure regardless of who is in office, but legal disputes arise with the exigencies of the day. The conservative legal movement will often, but not always, coalesce around current conservative political needs. Likewise, the conservative political movement will often, but not always, coalesce around the conservative legal movement. During two recent events, Justice Elena Kagan recognized this dichotomy. She observed that conservative members of the Supreme Court decide cases that are right "out of the playbook of the conservative legal movement," even as some of President Trump's policies suffer setbacks in the courts. Indeed, Trump has repeatedly expressed frustration that his appointees rule against him.

Many legal conservatives came to terms with voting for Trump in 2016 as a way to save the Supreme Court. Many more legal conservatives came around to Trump in 2020 based on his track record of appointing originalist judges--although I think that record is overstated. When Trump was on the ballot in 2024, some legal conservatives supported Trump for fear that Kamala Harris would pack the Supreme Court. But 2026 altered the calculus. Trump, and those candidates he endorsed, were routed in red states. Moreover, conservative judges and attorney generals were wiped from office. When the top of the ticket is no longer able to win elections, downballot judges and attorneys general do not stand a chance. Red states can only bleed for so long until they turn blue. A necessary condition for a successful legal movement is a political party that can produce viable candidates. Going forward, the path of advancing the conservative legal movement requires a political reorientation. The conservative legal movement can only survive if conservative lawyers are in public office to move the law. The blue tsunami cannot be allowed to drown originalism.

NEXT: School Districts' Negligence and Nuisance Claims Against Social Media Companies Rejected by California Court of Appeal

Josh Blackman is a constitutional law professor at the South Texas College of Law Houston and the President of the Harlan Institute. Follow him @JoshMBlackman.

2027 Mercedes-Benz Sprinter

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

See all results for new 2027 Mercedes-Benz Sprinter for sale near 98032

Seeing the Mercedes-Benz badge affixed to the front of a large commercial van feels, well, wrong, but the Sprinter van is a global leader in cargo hauling. It’s anything but a luxury-oriented Mercedes, instead focusing on package delivery and worksite practicality. A 170-hp diesel inline-four is standard, as is rear-wheel drive. A more powerful 211-hp version of the diesel is available and can be had with all-wheel drive.

The Sprinter is offered in several configurations: the Cargo Van model has seating for two and a crazy amount of cargo capacity behind the front seats. The Passenger Van variant can be had with up to five rows of seats to maximize people space. The Crew Van splits the difference and comes with two rows of seats with plenty of room behind the second row for your team’s tools of the trade.

Expert Tip: If you’re interested in an electric cargo van, Mercedes-Benz also makes the eSprinter, but we review that model separately.

Compare the 2027 Mercedes-Benz Sprinter with the top vehicles in this segment with our new compare tool.

The entry-level Worker trim for the Sprinter Cargo Van has been discontinued. Otherwise, the Sprinter carries over to 2027 without any other significant changes.

If this year's model is largely unchanged and you're comfortable with a lightly used car, you may want to consider shopping previous model years. A one-to-three-year-old model will likely have a warranty and it'll cost less than buying new.

Every Sprinter, save for the electric eSprinter, relies on diesel fuel. The base engine is a turbocharged 2.0-liter four-cylinder rated for 170 horsepower and 295 pound-feet of torque. The optional high-output engine is good for 211 ponies and 332 pound-feet. Both feed a nine-speed automatic transmission. All-wheel drive is available only with the higher-output engine.

While not as potent as the 310-hp EcoBoost V-6 in the Ford Transit or the 401-hp V-8 in the GMC Savana, the Sprinter's diesels offer adequate thrust. Though no van boasts razor-sharp handling, the Sprinter's standard load-adaptive stability control and available crosswind-assist systems help keep it out of trouble in dicey situations.

Car and Driver calculates your car's value in three free and easy steps - using the same Black Book® data dealers use to appraise vehicles.

In Comparison: Though we've yet to strap our test equipment to a recent Sprinter, we can tell you this is not a quick vehicle. We'd guess the base model would need 13.5 seconds to reach 60 mph, while the higher-output model might take 12.0. For reference, the Ford Transit can complete the same sprint in as little as 6.7 seconds.

The Sprinter can tow up to 7500 pounds when equipped with the high-output engine. Models with the 170-hp four-cylinder are good for 5000 pounds, though wheelbase and other specs can affect that cap.

The Sprinter's maximum payload is 6812 pounds. In order to reach that figure, you'll have to spec the cargo van with the heavy-duty 4500 chassis, the standard roof, the 144-inch wheelbase, rear-wheel drive, and the high-output engine. Crew models can carry as much as 3825 pounds, while the passenger van's hauling capacity tops out at 3307 pounds.

The EPA doesn't require automakers to submit fuel-economy data for full-size vans, nor does it publish such information. When we take a Sprinter on our 200-mile real-world highway fuel economy route, we'll update this section with the results.

First Row: Like other full-size vans, the entry-level Sprinter is spartan inside, but the driver's seat is heated, cruise control comes standard, and Benz offers several extra-cost upgrades to improve comfort, convenience, and driver safety.

Second, Third, Fourth, and Fifth Rows: The Sprinter cargo van has no seats after the driver and front passenger, but the Crew model gets a three-seat second row. Standard-length (144-inch) passenger vans can comfortably accommodate 12 people across four rows, while the 170-incher passenger van seats 15 across five rows.

Cargo Capacity: The cargo van offers up to 533 cubic feet of space for whatever you need to haul. Crew vans can swallow a max of 396 cubes, while the passenger van (when fully occupied) tops out at 117 cubic feet.

The Sprinter is not your typical Mercedes. It's built for utility first and foremost, which helps explain the base model's underwhelming tech setup. Mercedes provides an AM/FM radio that plays through five speakers (13 in passenger vans).

Shoppers can option a 10.3-inch touchscreen, though, and that system supports wireless Apple CarPlay and Android Auto connections.

The majority of the Sprinter's driver-assistance features are optional, not standard.

For information about the Sprinter's crash-test results, visit the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) and Insurance Institute for Highway Safety (IIHS) websites.

The Sprinter's limited warranty is typical for the class, but its powertrain coverage is longer than most. Mercedes doesn't include scheduled maintenance.

Grab This Trader Joe's Fast Food Dupe For A High-Protein Breakfast

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Trader Joe's is always showing you things you didn't know you needed until you set foot inside the store. A plethora of gummy candies? Check. Enough tasty frozen dinners to keep you from using your stove for a month? Absolutely. A variety of frozen items for less than $5? Without a doubt. Though they didn't make the cut on our official list of best frozen goodies, included in that last category are its English Muffin Breakfast Sandwiches, a high-protein fast food dupe that is a savory treat for your morning meal, or any time you need a quick, filling snack. Better still, you'll be able to tell your family that we have McDonald's Sausage McMuffins with Egg in the freezer.

Much like the aforementioned Sausage McMuffin with Egg, the TJ's version, which sells in a box of two for $4.99, has both a sausage and egg patty and a bit of melted cheddar cheese, placed between two slices of English muffin. And while it doesn't quite have the same amount of protein — 18 grams to the McDonald's sandwich's 20 — it nonetheless provides a very reasonable amount of the macronutrient, which is anywhere from 10 to over 30% of the average recommended daily intake.

In addition to keeping you feeling fuller longer as you go about your morning, these Trader Joe's breakfast sandwiches are also a joy to eat. One commenter on Reddit shared that they would definitely buy them again, writing that while they could make their own version, "these are quick, easy and [have] minimal cleanup[, so they're] perfect for a busy morning." We'd also add that you don't have to wait in a drive-thru line for them, either.

There are indeed heating instructions on the box of the Trader Joe's English Muffin Breakfast Sandwiches, for both the microwave and the air fryer. However, people on the internet have tried following the directions and ended up with results that were ... less than satisfactory. For one, we'd recommend skipping the microwave altogether; this method of heating food and bread products doesn't tend to work very well (bread usually emerges from the microwave tough and chewy). We know: It's the quickest method, and if it's all you have time for, go ahead and use it, but if you're not in a huge rush, the air fryer is much better (and you don't have to worry about anything dripping through the perforations, like you would with batter-y or runny foods).

That said, even the air fryer instructions might require a little tweaking, as one person on that same Reddit thread wrote that they tried cooking them in the super-hot appliance according to the directions, but that it needed a minute or so less than instructed. It's also important to note that if you want to heat up the contents of the box, which are two breakfast sandwiches, and you can't fit both sandwiches in your basket open-faced (which is the suggested way of cooking them), heat them one at a time for best results, rather than crowding the cooking surface.

Lastly, some report the sarnies need a little snazzing up. With that in mind, we suggest adding a dash of your favorite hot sauce, or maybe even a little ketchup or barbecue, just to add a little interest. It is a perfectly blank canvas, after all!

Heliene and Caelux combine PV cells and solar glass for a hybrid module

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Heliene, a solar modules manufacturer, and Caelux Corporation, a perovskite solar technology company, are starting commercial production of their Hybrid-Tandem solar module.

This module combines perovskite glass from Caelux and silicon N-type cells from Heliene, placing two energy generating sources in a single solar panel. Heliene is producing the modules in its plant in Mountain Iron, Minnesota, with first deliveries slated for 2027.

“One of the advantages of Caelux’s technology is that we can integrate its Energy Producing Glass into our existing manufacturing lines without having to build an entirely new production setup,” said Martin Pochtaruk, CEO and founder of Heliene. “With both companies manufacturing in the U.S., we’re bringing together two fully domestic supply chains to advance what’s possible with American-made solar, besides the fact that partnering with true friends also makes it fun.”

The companies are reporting a 27% efficiency from the Hybrid-Tandem module. Heliene and Caelux desiged this module to have a higher power density in a smaller project footprint.

“Heliene is a true leader in U.S. manufacturing, and we share a belief that when we lead the market, our customers win. Together, we’re commercializing ultra-high-efficiency solar modules that will set a new bar for American-made solar,” said Caelux CEO Scott Graybeal.

Heliene will showcase the Hybrid-Tandem module at RE+ in Las Vegas from Nov. 17 to 19, at booth #W16546.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

Near AI Breach Exposes User Details and Data From 18 AI Agents

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Near AI Breach Exposes User Details and Data From 18 AI Agents

Near AI has disclosed a breach that exposed account information belonging to about 100 users and data from 18 AI agents after an attacker exploited software running its servers.

The company said in a November 9 report that an attacker used a remote code execution vulnerability in SGLang, an open-source engine that runs AI models and generates responses to user requests.

The exploit allowed the intruder to execute commands on one inference server and then reach other parts of Near AI’s infrastructure.

Investigators traced the intrusion to an internal system connected to Private Chat, a feature the company had already retired. The attacker downloaded names and email addresses for approximately 100 users, along with subscription plan and account balance information for some of them.

Another 18 agents were accessed on an older deployment of Near AI Agents, where the attacker retrieved their chat histories, settings and logs. Those agents were not running inside NEAR AI’s confidential computing environment, which is designed to shield information from the company operating the servers.

Logs also exposed NEAR AI Cloud API keys belonging to two customers. These keys function like digital credentials that applications use to access cloud services; Near AI says it has revoked both, but did not say whether the attacker used them.

Near AI found no indication that stored Private Chat conversations or uploaded files had been accessed. The confirmed theft involved account records and legacy agent data, while exposure of live confidential requests remains a possibility identified by the investigation.

Near AI is part of the broader Near ecosystem, which has been promoting AI privacy alongside its blockchain ambitions. Its Cloud product uses trusted execution environments, or TEEs, to keep AI prompts encrypted and isolated from server operators, even while models process them.

Hardware isolation does not guarantee that the software inside the protected environment is safe. “It does not protect against a vulnerability in the software running inside the environment,” Near AI acknowledged, explaining that the SGLang flaw let the attacker run code where confidential requests were being processed.

That is particularly awkward for a business selling privacy as a core feature. In October, Near AI announced that Brave’s Leo browser assistant was using its confidential inference infrastructure for a supported AI model, although the incident report does not identify Brave users among those affected.

SGLang had already attracted scrutiny from security researchers. In July, Carnegie Mellon University’s CERT Coordination Center documented six vulnerabilities in the project, including bugs that could let attackers execute commands or expose credentials, depending on how servers were configured.

Near AI did not identify the specific SGLang flaw exploited in this incident, so there is no basis to link the breach to any of those earlier disclosures. It reported the vulnerability to SGLang’s developers.

Near, to its credit, was quick to fix the issues.

“On detection, we revoked all active sessions and administrative credentials, replaced all agent tokens, took Private Chat offline, and deployed a fix for the SGLang vulnerability across all inference servers,” the project said.

”We've completed the rotation of all remaining credentials and a full forensic review. We have notified users whose account details or agents we have confirmed were accessed and will make any regulatory notifications that are required.”

Near AI notified users whose account records or agents it confirmed were accessed and says it will make any required regulatory notifications. It also warned users to be wary of unsolicited messages referring to their Near AI accounts or balances, and contacted the owners of all 18 affected agents individually.

AI firms sign Macron’s Paris Call, but safety group says it lacks teeth

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

OpenAI, Anthropic and Microsoft promise shutdown switches and outside testing, but there are no shared risk limits.

The Paris Call meeting at the Élysée Palace on 9 November, with leaders and AI executives joining by video

OpenAI, Anthropic, Microsoft and Mistral are among ten AI companies that have signed a new declaration on the safety of advanced AI models. The Paris Call for AI Safety and Security was agreed on Monday at a meeting hosted by French President Emmanuel Macron.

L’intelligence artificielle avance très vite. On l’a déjà vu : cela implique des risques réels pour notre sécurité collective.

Notre travail pour developper nos technologies doit aussi être celui de l’anticipation et de la maîtrise de ses risques.

J’ai réuni à Paris et en visio une cinquantaine d’acteurs : chefs d’État et de gouvernement, dirigeants des plus grands laboratoires d’IA, chercheurs et experts.

Le constat est partagé. Il faut agir ensemble. Nous avons obtenu des engagements de tous autour de trois priorités : mieux évaluer de façon indépendante et publique les risques des modèles les plus avancés, mieux partager les alertes en cas d’incident grave et responsabiliser les acteurs qui développent des modèles.

Notre ambition est claire : faire de l’IA un progrès pour tous, avec une sécurité garantie par l’engagement de chacun.

— Emmanuel Macron (@EmmanuelMacron) November 9, 2026

The announcement came on the opening day of the Paris Peace Forum, where AI was one of the main topics of discussion.

Six countries signed the declaration: France, Canada, Finland, Norway, the Netherlands and Bahrain. The other participating companies are Hugging Face, Cohere, Black Forest Labs, Domyn, Sakana AI and Synthesia. Yoshua Bengio’s AI safety organisation LawZero is among the research groups supporting it.

Around 50 people joined the discussions by video, including OpenAI chief executive Sam Altman, Anthropic chief executive Dario Amodei and Microsoft president Brad Smith.

US Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent also took part, although the US is not among the signatories. Google, Meta and China have not signed the declaration either.

Macron said AI was advancing rapidly and posed “real risks to our collective security”, calling for governments and companies to work together to address them.

The signatories have committed to developing fail-safe mechanisms that can shut down their AI models, including during training and internal use. They will also provide clearer information about what their systems can and cannot do, and allow qualified independent experts to test them where appropriate.

The declaration also calls for a shared approach to assessing and managing the risks of frontier AI. This would cover malicious uses, including cyberattacks and weapons development, as well as the possibility of AI systems acting independently or moving beyond human control. Non-sensitive test results would be published in a common format.

Governments and companies will also share early warnings about serious incidents, while whistleblowers will have access to secure channels for reporting concerns.

The commitments have drawn criticism from French AI safety organisation CeSIA, which signed the declaration but argues that it leaves important gaps.

It sets no common risk thresholds for deciding whether a model should be developed or released, and does not guarantee that independent testers or governments will have access to the systems they need to assess.

CeSIA also wants governments to have the authority to suspend a model when its risks become too high. It argues that developers need clearer obligations and a timetable for meeting them.

“These decisions must rest with public authorities,” said Charbel-Raphaël Ségerie, CeSIA’s executive director.

The Paris Call follows an initiative launched by Norway and Finland in September to bring frontier AI under greater oversight, which France later joined. It also builds on earlier global safety commitments made by AI companies in Seoul and discussions at the G7 summit hosted by France in Evian.

The declaration remains open to other signatories, but it sets no deadlines. The shared testing framework it proposes has yet to be developed.

On Tuesday, the forum turned to another concern for Macron: the effects of AI on children. Speaking at a summit on children in the AI era, the French president called for AI tools to undergo checks before being made available to young users.

“We will push for pre-certification of all artificial intelligence solutions,” he said.

Macron compared the idea to the safety checks required for children’s toys, arguing that parents would not give a child an untested toy and that AI tools should be held to the same standard.

He also warned that the effects of AI on young people’s mental health were already becoming apparent, as more children and teenagers turn to chatbots that can influence their emotions and how they see the world. A charter on safer AI for children was due to be signed later on Tuesday.

President Emmanuel Macron also received a report, titled “Protect without preventing”, from a government-appointed panel of experts examining generative AI and human vulnerability.

Mauricio Pochettino Appointment Can Save Lisandro Martinez's Man Utd Career

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Manchester United are in a crisis, and the board look set to make the decision that a change is necessary.

United sit 18th in the Premier League with just one win in 10 Premier League games so far this season, while they have won just 3 of 14 in all competitions under Carrick, and GIVEMESPORT sources understand that the iconic former midfielder is set to be relieved of his duties at Old Trafford as a result.

Speculation has been rife for several weeks about his future, and who could potentially replace him, and it has now emerged that USMNT boss Mauricio Pochettino is currently a leading candidate for the role.

The Argentine has got plenty of Premier League and Champions League experience, having managed Southampton, Tottenham, Paris Saint-Germain, and Chelsea before taking up his role as boss of the United States, where he led the team to the Last 16 at the 2026 World Cup, before being rewarded with a new four-year deal.

But that contract is understood to have a $10m (£7.4m) release clause, which could open the door for him to return to England once again, and finally get the Man Utd job he has been pining for since 2019.

His arrival would be a mark of intent from INEOS, and potentially a final roll of the dice for board members like Jason Wilcox and Omar Berrada, who have so far failed to live up to their reputation of being 'best in class' since being brought in.

However, on the pitch, his appointment could also be a saving grace for some players or a dagger in the heart for others, who may not fit in with his style of play or how he sees football.

One player who could potentially be handed a reprieve at Old Trafford under the 54-year-old is his fellow countryman, Lisandro Martinez, who has been strongly linked with an exit away from the club in 2027.

The central defender has been in terrible form in recent weeks, having been at fault for a number of goals, before a ridiculous red card against Chelsea led to the team's collapse in a 3-0 defeat at Stamford Bridge. But more than that was his public outburst against the club hierarchy, where he admitted to feeling let down by the summer business and the strength of the squad. He has been labelled as being part of a 'toxic' culture at the club by pundit Adam Joseph.

Martinez is out of contract at Man Utd at the end of the season, and while the club have the option to extend by a further 12 months, reports have suggested they will not be looking to trigger that.

But with Pochettino being a manager who likes to dominate possession and build up from the back, the quality that Martinez has and his leadership qualities will likely be huge for the coach when it comes to implementing his style in the short-term.

Martinez had been a firm fan favourite until this season, and should he be able to string together a run of top performances and United start to turn the season around, he would likely be back in the good books quickly. That could force INEOS' hand when it comes to his contract situation, and could also convince the player that he is at the right club after all.

With funds understood to be limited, finding a replacement immediately for Martinez will be difficult. Pochettino won't want to be handicapped anymore if he is stepping in mid-season, and that could be the perfect remedy for a reprieve.

I’ve waited a long time for the Boox Picco, and I’m not disappointed

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

At around $100, the Boox Picco offers one of the most portable e-reader experiences you can get. You add MagSafe-like capability with a refreshingly simple UI, and you have a delightful way to read, literally wherever you are.

There is an argument to be made for every single shape and size e-readers come in, and they come in so many different sizes it’s hard to keep track. I love the idea of having a 10-inch display that I can skim, but even if it’s perfect for sitting at home and reading anything from novels to comics, I don’t think it provides a very good travel experience

But even smaller devices like the Boox Palma make you choose between bringing a second device or sticking with your phone. Most often, the phone wins out – especially when you have a flexible approach with a foldable like the Galaxy Z Fold 8 I’ve been using daily.

Unlike the other phone-sized e-readers from Boox, the Picco stands at just 4.15 inches tall. The screen measures at just under that – 3.97 inches. It’s essentially the size of a MagSafe or Qi2 wallet – just small enough to fit on the back of your phone with the included magnetic case.

It’s thin, too. A 5.5mm side profile means it slips anywhere a minimal wallet would without issue. That goes for pockets just as well as crossbody bags; perfect for travel.

And that’s where I think I find so much more joy in using this over what would otherwise be considered a small e-reader, like the Palma. I don’t have a lot of excuses not to carry it around when I’m on the go. I’ll throw it in my Bellroy bag when I’m carrying what I’d considered my minimal EDC. It slides into any internal pocket, and it weighs next to nothing.

The back of the Boox Picco isn’t magnetic, but the included case is. If you want the thinnest possible footprint, forego the case. But adding it lets you do one of the coolest – and slightly copium-inducing – things with your phone: attach the e-ink device directly to your OLED-laden phone.

It does not work well with an unconventional device like my Galaxy Z Fold 8, but if you have nearly any other device like the Pixel 11, you should get a rather perfect fit. The Picco squares up to the back of any slab phone with Qi2 magnets, whether internal or via a case.

But, why? Why slap another device and add weight/thickness to your phone when it’s perfectly capable of displaying e-books?

Because it’s cool! That’s the driving reason. You can make the argument that it doubles your phone’s usability or that it gives you an easy place to store the tiny e-reader. The truth is that it does add extra thickness to your phone, and using your phone with the Picco piggybacking along can be uncomfortable, since your fingers now rest on another display and not a case or phone backing.

You can get used to it, and, as unnecessary as it may seem, it’s a fun way to carry around a much more eye-friendly screen.

The Picco diverts from other Boox devices in another way. It doesn’t run Android, which means you have no access to third-party apps like Google Play Books, Amazon Kindle, or any other store/library. Instead, the Picco uses a companion app to transfer EPUBs from your phone to the tiny e-reader.

I initially opposed this idea when I got a sneak peek during IFA in Berlin this year. But after using it for a few weeks, I’ll admit I’ve come around. But that all comes down to how you consume your media.

I try to source EPUBs no matter what device I use. On Android-powered e-readers, that means downloading Google Play Books and reading from there; I have my EPUBs uploaded to my own Google Play Books library, so they’re available universally.

On the Picco, my approach hasn’t changed much. I take those same EPUBs I have in storage and transfer them from the companion app.

I can understand how the lack of third-party libraries would make the Boox Picco moot. Still, I’ve been a proponent of buying digital books I can store in my own drives, so I can read them wherever, whether that’s the Google Play Books library or from a device like the Picco.

All of that is a roundabout way of saying the Picco, though it doesn’t let you use apps to read, makes it easy to get digital copies loaded and ready to flip through.

It uses a handshake connection that requires you to open the companion app on your phone, hit connect, and do the same on the Picco. Once connected, transferring EPUB files is as easy as selecting them from your phone’s physical or cloud storage and queueing them up. The process takes seconds, and the only issue I’ve had was solved by a force stop and trying again.

The homescreen is simply a carousel of your library, with a delightful little progress bar under each title as they’re sorted. You can access and manage the bookshelf from that page, as well as the minimal “apps” and settings. I appreciate the focus app and reading stats, but the countdown and to-do options require using the companion app. I don’t see the point of those.

It doesn’t even have a control panel. Swiping down from the top surfaces screen brightness and color controls. Even though this little guy is just over $100, it still has a front light controlled by a physical switch on the side. Compare that to e-readers at much higher price points without that game-changing feature.

The buttons opposite don’t control volume. Instead, they’re there for page turning.

It’s just simple and well-designed. Features are there to make reading a better experience, down to granular text controls for your digital books. It does exactly what it needs to do – nothing more, nothing less.

As far as battery goes, it meets my expectations. It’s not massive, so the battery is small in comparison to a smartphone. It still holds its own easily, with an easy week of use if you’re reading for an hour or two a day. Beyond that, it takes little time to charge and does great on idle, which is perfect for such a passive device.

The Picco will hit home for you if you want an ultra-portable option for your e-books. The same goes for those who don’t mind the smaller screen and lack of Android or third-party app support.

I get genuine joy every time I pick it up to read where I left off, and I think that’s because it’s so simple and nothing about it feels bloated, even the companion app, which is also straight to the point.

I think it’s been a while since a device has offered that experience for me. Adding the form factor, optional magnetic attachment for your phone, and price tag just gets me to that conclusion sooner.

The Boox Picco is the most adorable e-reader I’ve used, and it’s $109 on Amazon.

Epic responds after Gears of War creator claims it won’t give back the first character he created

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Epic Games refused to hand Gears of War creator Cliff Bleszinski the rights to Jazz Jackrabbit, though he claimed neither side had the paperwork.

The hare hasn’t exactly been gathering dust, either. More than three decades after Bleszinski first doodled him, Epic slotted Jazz into Fortnite Chapter 7 Season 4 as the Jackrabbit Sprite, which grants players an extra mid-air jump. So when his creator came asking for him back, Epic dug in.

In a November 7 post on X, Bleszinski wrote: “After lawyering up Epic claims they fully own the rights to Jazz Jackrabbit and won’t back down in spite of not having any paperwork to prove it (nor do I).”

Still, he pointed out that Epic pays him “from sales of it on GoG (which means paperwork exists somewhere)” and is “still happy to monetize him in FN,” referring to Fortnite.

Epic, however, stood by its position. In a statement to Dexerto, the company said: “Epic owns the Jazz Jackrabbit IP. We pay 100% of revenue generated by the Jazz Jackrabbit games on Gog.com to the four individuals who developed the game, including Cliff Bleszinski.”

The statement didn’t address the missing paperwork or his claim that he created the character before joining the company.

To make his case, Bleszinski also dredged up his first high school sketches of the gun-toting rabbit, drawn when he was around 17. When Limited Run Games CEO Josh Fairhurst asked whether the doodles proved the character predated Epic, Bleszinski replied that one would think so.

He joined Epic in 1992, and the rollicking platformer, coded by Arjan Brussee, launched in 1994 as the studio’s biggest seller at the time. Today, the game’s GOG listing names Epic as developer and publisher and identifies Jazz Jackrabbit as an Epic trademark.

In a statement to IGN, Bleszinski said, “Jazz was my first hit and will always have a spot in my heart.” Even so, he said any future Jazz-like project would have to take the spiritual sequel route. “Business is business, I suppose,” he wrote.

A spiritual sequel would join a growing wishlist, after Bleszinski revealed earlier this year that he still wants to make a game that mixes John Wick and Carrie.

Ghana vs Somalia - prediction, team news, lineups

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

By Joshua Ojele | 10 Nov 2026 20:14, Last updated: 10 Nov 2026 20:24

Ghana will be desperate to breathe life into their 2027 Africa Cup of Nations qualifying campaign when they play host to Somalia at the Essipong Sports Stadium in the first of their Group C double-header on Thursday.

Having lost their first two matches, both nations are left with little room for mistakes and know Thursday’s result could prove decisive in shaping up their qualifying ambitions, and this makes for an exciting contest in Sekondi-Takoradi.

Two games into the race for a spot in the 2027 AFCON, Ghana find themselves amongst nine sides yet to pick up any points in the qualifiers, with the federation parting ways with Portuguese coach Carlos Queiroz and opting for a change in ideas in the dugout.

Coming off the back of a round-of-32 defeat against Colombia in the World Cup back in June, the Black Stars kicked off their AFCON qualifying campaign with a 2-0 loss against Ivory Coast on September 24, with Malick Yalcouye and Franck Kessie netting on either side of half-time to hand the Elephants all three points.

Playing in front of the home supporters five days later, Ghana failed to make home advantage count in matchday two, when they fell to a 4-2 defeat at the hands of Gambia after squandering a two-goal lead at the Accra Sports Stadium.

Ernest Nuamah and Felix Afena-Gyan hit the target to put Ghana in the driver’s seat, but strikes from Muhammed Badamosi, Omar Colley, and a second-half brace from Konyaspor striker Ebrima Colley turned the game on its head for Gambia.

The four-time African champions have now gone eight straight games without a win in the Africa Cup of Nations qualifiers, a run which saw them miss out on the 2025 AFCON, failing to feature in the continental showpiece for the first time since 2004.

Now tasked with restoring confidence and flipping the script, interim coach Kim Lars Bjorkegren faces a must-win assignment on Thursday as Ghana seek to avoid missing back-to-back AFCON tournaments for the first time since their three successive absences in 1986, 1988 and 1990.

Like the home side, Somalia journey to the Essipong Sports Stadium with the same urgent need for a result if they are to turn their fortunes around and secure a first-ever qualification for the Africa Cup of Nations.

Having edged out Eswatini and Mauritius in the preliminary phase, the Ocean Stars fell to a 2-1 loss against Gambia in the Group C opener on September 25, with Ebrima Colley and Muhammed Badamosi netting on either side of Bilal Njie’s 72nd-minute equaliser to hand the hosts all three points at the Independence Stadium.

While there were positives to take from the defeat against Gambia, Somalia were left spitting feathers four days later, when they fell to a 2-0 defeat against Ivory Coast through strikes from Yann Gboho and Bazoumana Toure in the final six minutes at the Stade Felix Houphouet-Boigny.

Yusuf Ali’s men demonstrated their ability to rise to the occasion against quality opposition last time out when they pushed Ivory Coast to the limit, and they will have reason to believe they can upset the odds against a Ghana side currently far from convincing.

Ghana Africa Cup of Nations Qualifying form:

Somalia Africa Cup of Nations Qualifying form:

Newly-appointed Ghana boss Bjorkegren has called up 26 players to the squad for October’s double-header with Somalia, including the Premier League trio of Abdul Fatawu Issahaku, Brandon Thomas-Asante and Ibrahim Osman.

Having missed the first two qualifying matches through a groin problem, Jordan Ayew has been recalled to the squad, and the nation’s captain will hope his leadership and wealth of experience can count for something.

The Black Stars will also be lifted by the return of Tottenham Hotspur forward Mohammed Kudus, who is set to make his first international appearance of the year after missing the World Cup through an injury.

Manchester City forward Antoine Semenyo misses out through a knee injury, while 31-year-old defender Jan Gyamerah, who made his debut against Mexico on May 26, is also ruled out through a muscle problem.

Before their late collapse, Somalia held their own against a superior and more experienced Ivory Coast side last time out, and we expect Ali to name a similar XI on Thursday, with Mohamed Mohamed, Abel Gigli, Ali Omar and Faisal Othman shielding 20-year-old Ibrahim Ahmed Hussein between the sticks.

With eight goal involvements in his last nine matches for club and country, KFUM Oslo striker Nije is currently in fine form, and the 28-year-old striker should lead the Ocean Stars attack once again, while Mohamed Omar, Abdi Sharif and Mukhtar Suleiman should team up in midfield.

Although Somalia have shown glimpses of their potential in their opening two matches, the difference in individual quality could prove a decisive factor here, with Ghana tipped to come away with all three points in front of their home supporters.

Actor Says He Removed His Mother’s Art from Venice Biennale Installation as a Pro-Israel Protest

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Actor Eric Rasmussen revealed this week that he had removed his contribution to an installation by Denniston Hill in the main exhibition of the Venice Biennale because the residency program appeared to support the “eradication” of Israel, in Rasmussen’s view.

In an op-ed published by the Jewish Journal on Monday, Rasmussen wrote that he had combined two drawings by his mother of Adolf Hitler drawn from a notebook she kept during her childhood. The resulting work, transferred to an acetate panel, was included Denniston Hill’s installation, an exquisite corpse–style work titled Chimera (2026) that is located in the Giardini section of Koyo Kouoh’s main exhibition. Related Articles French Artist Claude Lévêque Ordered to Stand Trial for Rape and Sexual Assault of Minors Venice Biennale President Stands by Decision to Host Russian Pavilion: 'I'd Do It All Over Again'

The piece features contributions by dozens of past residents at Denniston Hill, which was cofounded by artists kara lynch, Julie Mehretu, Paul Pfeiffer, and Jessica Rankin, along with art historian Lawrence Chua and curator Beth Stryker, and is located in Upstate New York. Rasmussen was a resident at Denniston Hill in 2019 through the DPAA Workshop.

Rasmussen said his submission to Chimera was a 1943 drawing by his mother that he had previously enlisted in a performance staged during his Denniston Hill residency. The piece is a drawing of a mustachioed man and some numbers, along with text reading, “Mister Hitler is invaded and his spitting.” For the Venice Biennale, he added a caption in red: “Mom’s childhood drawing 1943. The Toxicity of the Past?”

“I felt like the piece reflected the work I did at Denniston Hill while, at the same time, gesturing toward our current moment,” Rasmussen writes. “Neither the ‘toxicity’ of authoritarian leaders nor the rabid antisemitism called to mind by Hitler’s image remain in the past.”

ARTnews was able to confirm that the drawing did make it on view in time for the Biennale’s opening in May by examining installation shots of Chimera disseminated by the exhibition, which closes later this month.

It appears to have been taken away by Rasmussen at some point after mid-May, when, he wrote, Denniston Hill sent an email noting that the organization was more than 70 Biennale participants who had signed an open letter that objected to the Biennale’s claims of political neutrality. The signatories said this conflicted with the controversial decision this year to host pavilions for Israel, Russia, and the United States, all of which are “current regimes committing war crimes,” the letter said.

Moreover, according to Rasmussen, Denniston Hill wrote that it was “aligning with ANGA,” or the Art Not Genocide Alliance, a group that has repeatedly protested Israel’s presence at the Biennale while also noting that Palestine has no official pavilion in the show, and never has. (Palestine is not considered a state in Italy; only countries viewed as states by Italy’s government can have national pavilions at the Biennale, per the exhibition.)

“ANGA clearly considers all of Israel to be occupied Palestine and its goal is not to oppose policies of the government of Israel but to destroy the nation,” Rasmussen claimed in his Jewish Journal essay, which alleged that Denniston Hill has “no problem blithely aligning itself with an organization that seeks that nation’s eradication without even asking for the opinions of its artists.”

His essay also labeled anti-Zionism “an assault on not only the Jewish state and Jewish history, but also on language itself,” and claimed that linking “Zionism with racism, colonialism and apartheid” was akin to “Cold War KGB propaganda.”

Without stating a precise date for the work’s removal, Rasmussen said he “wrote a lengthy email” to Denniston Hill seeking the return of the drawing. Denniston Hill obliged the request and gave back the drawing, he said.

Representatives for Denniston Hill and the Biennale did not respond to requests for comment.

Nvidia board hires ex-prosecutor Joon Kim to investigate Jensen Huang

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The Nvidia board running the inquiry has no chair and no compliance chief, and its lead director once ran the company’s law firm. Three of Huang’s relatives work there.

NVIDIA founder and CEO Jensen Huang during his GTC 2026 keynote at the SAP Center in San Jose, 16 March 2026

Nvidia’s independent directors have hired the law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to investigate chief executive Jensen Huang. The board retained Joon H. Kim, a former acting US Attorney in Manhattan, and his firm to run it. The board announced the choice on Tuesday, CNBC reported.

“The independent members of the NVIDIA Board have retained former acting U.S. Attorney of the Southern District of New York Joon H. Kim and his firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to conduct a thorough and independent investigation as was announced on Sunday,” the board said in a statement.

The board opened its own inquiry after Huang admitted an affair with Alice Huang, a former executive at the chip buyer Megaspeed. US intelligence agencies have concluded she was a spy for China, which has rejected the claims. Huang would not take part in choosing counsel and would keep leading the company, a spokesman had said, according to the Times.

The Nvidia board has a combined 161 years of service, Kalley Huang and Don Clark reported for The New York Times. Huang, Tench Coxe and Harvey Jones have sat on it since 1993, according to Nvidia’s proxy statement.

Sequoia Capital and Sutter Hill Ventures led a $2m round in Nvidia in 1993. Their investors, Mark Stevens and Coxe, then joined the board. Stevens left in 2006 and returned in 2008, the proxy shows. Brooke Seawell, a former Synopsys finance chief, joined in 1997. Jones and Seawell held Nvidia shares worth a combined $1.86bn as of June, the Times reported.

Before the hire, people familiar with the inquiry told the Financial Times it would probably be led by Stephen Neal, the lead independent director. Neal ran the law firm Cooley from 2001 to 2008 and is still its senior counsel. Cooley set up Nvidia in 1993 and has been its main outside counsel since, CNBC reported, citing the firm. Neal did not respond to CNBC.

Average director tenure is almost 15 years, nearly twice the S&P 500 norm, Semafor reported. No director is a current or former S&P 500 chief executive, and none has national security experience. None of the directors responded to the Times.

Nvidia has a lead independent director instead of a board chair. The proxy says this gives each member “an equal voice”. Huang told Bloomberg’s Emily Chang that he skipped the chairman role so the chief executive could be held accountable, Bloomberg reported.

“Unlike many CEOs, I’m completely fireable,” Huang said.

Nvidia’s code of conduct tells staff and directors to avoid any activity that conflicts with the company’s interests, the proxy says. That includes anything that looks like a conflict. The company does not have a chief compliance officer, the filing confirms. Nvidia has said a committee of its finance, operations and human resources heads checks that staff follow its policies, Fortune reported.

Nvidia’s annual report does not name Huang as a key person. It says the company depends heavily on its longstanding executive team.

“Failure to ensure effective succession planning, transfer of knowledge, and smooth transitions involving executives and key employees could hinder our strategic planning, execution, and long-term success,” the filing says.

Huang is Nvidia’s co-founder, chief executive and president. He has 33 direct reports this week, according to internal records reviewed by CNBC. He told CNBC in September 2025 that he had as many as 60. The proxy says the senior managers who work directly with him give the board a pool of candidates for promotion.

Huang owned 3.58% of Nvidia in March, the proxy shows, and he has no special voting rights, the Financial Times reported. He told Bloomberg he hoped the next chief executive already worked at Nvidia. In January, he said a chief executive who does not do the job “is let go”, the Times reported. He added a second thought.

“There will never be one like me, and the reason for that is because I was raised by the company,” Huang said.

Three relatives work at Nvidia. His son Spencer is a director of product management in robotics, and his daughter Madison is a senior director of product marketing, according to the Times. His son-in-law, Nico Caprez, works on business development, the Times reported. Fortune gives his title as vice president of global AI infrastructure growth.

Nvidia paid Madison about $1.23m and Spencer about $1.32m in its 2026 financial year, the proxy shows. Neither is an executive officer, shares a household with Huang or reports directly to him. Their pay was set under the company’s normal practices without Huang’s involvement, the filing says.

Asked last month whether Madison was in line to succeed him, Huang said he did not wish the job on her. She cancelled a trip to Seoul on Monday, citing a “family matter”, according to Bloomberg. Huang asked for privacy for them in his memo to staff.

“My family bears no responsibility for my choices. I do. Please give them privacy,” he wrote.

Nvidia reports its third-quarter results on 17 November. A day later, House committee chairs expect Huang at a closed-door briefing on Megaspeed.

Stolen McLaren 750S Spotted Doing Donuts at Detroit Street Takeover, Tracked Down Via Social Media

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The McLaren's owner put his 1.1 million followers to work tracking down his purloined supercar.

If you’re going to steal a bright green McLaren 750S, you might want to try to keep a low profile. At least, as low a profile as can be possible in a supercar. Doing donuts at a street takeover in front of a crowd of spectators, then, is probably not the best idea.

According to ClickOnDetroit, this indeed happened. The McLaren in question belonged to Ali Sobh, a Detroit businessman and social media influencer with approximately 1.1 million followers. The exotic car was reportedly stolen from a driveway behind his home in Detroit's historic Boston-Edison neighborhood. Sobh was understandably upset when he realized his prized possession was taken; however, salt arrived in the wound when he saw a video circulating online showing his McLaren spinning donuts in front of spectators, many of whom were recording the spectacle on their phones.

So, Sobh turned to his social media followers for help locating the McLaren. Tips began coming in, eventually providing a possible location near Six Mile Road and Telegraph Road. Sobh passed that information along to Detroit police, who subsequently recovered the car.

Getting the McLaren back is obviously a big win, but that’s not the end of the story. Detroit police are reviewing footage from the street takeover and working to identify those responsible for the theft. Authorities have not announced any arrests, and it's unclear whether the person performing donuts was involved in stealing the vehicle.

Sobh claims his car is worth approximately $500,000, and he doesn’t ever want it stolen again, so he says plans to improve security around his home to prevent that from happening. In the meantime, it must be reassuring to know that he can count on more than one million followers to help him out when things go south.

Fitness influencer who overcame anorexia to become bodybuilding champion dies at 26

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Fitness influencer and two-time bodybuilding champion Alix Small has died at the age of 26, years after overcoming anorexia to compete on one of the sport’s biggest stages.

The Scottish athlete, originally from Forfar, died on November 8, with news of her death confirmed by her mother, Karen Small, and a close friend.

Bodybuilding news account Beyond The Stage TV announced her death on Instagram, writing: “Shocked to hear to the terrible news of the passing of 2 x Fitness Pro winner and Olympian alixsmallll.”

The account added that it had received confirmation from Small’s mother and best friend.

According to TMZ, her cause of death has not been disclosed, and the circumstances surrounding her death remain unclear.

A post shared by BTSTV (@beyondthestagetv_)

Small began struggling with anorexia when she was just 15 years old, with her weight reportedly dropping to around 70 pounds before she discovered bodybuilding.

In a 2021 interview with STV News, she credited the sport with helping her recovery by shifting her focus away from appearance and toward physical strength.

“People think bodybuilding is an aesthetics sport and it is but you’ve got to let go of what you look like and it’s about the performance side of it,” she explained.

“That’s really helped me along my journey and I just want to be the best that I can be.”

Small earned her IFBB Pro card at just 19 years old and won the 2021 Europa Pro Championships in Spain, qualifying for the prestigious Olympia competition, where she finished 12th.

This Billionaire Invested in OpenAI in 2019. Here’s What He’s Betting on Next.

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

This billionaire is betting that AI will revamp websites as we know them.

He made billions betting on what’s next. Now this 71-year-old investor’s latest move reflects his conviction that AI will change how the Internet works.

Vinod Khosla’s venture capital firm, Khosla Ventures, made early investments in OpenAI, DoorDash and Instacart. His latest investment, AI startup Coframe, targets another potential shift: how websites operate. Coframe wants to rewire the Internet so every visitor to a website sees a page custom-made for them.

“The world has changed because AI has enabled extreme customization,” Khosla said in an interview with Business Insider. Forbes reported earlier this year that Khosla’s net worth is around $10 billion.

Coframe announced this week that it raised $35 million in total funding, including a $25 million Series A led by Khosla Ventures and Hanabi Capital. Executives at Anthropic, OpenAI and Google DeepMind also participated.

Coframe helps businesses improve their websites so more people buy something, sign up for something or take another desired action. The startup creates and tests different versions of a webpage, including text, images and design. It then tracks how visitors interact with the webpage to determine how to improve it.

For example, say an online store wants to sell more shoes. Coframe could help the company test different headlines, product images or page layouts and determine which versions lead to more purchases.

“For decades, the web has been frozen in place, showing every visitor essentially the same page,” Khosla said in the press release. “Coframe is building the living internet, where two people can visit the same website and have completely different experiences, personalized to who they are and what they need. These interfaces continuously learn and improve themselves. This is a huge leap beyond traditional A/B testing and the next iteration of the internet.”

Coframe claims that it can help businesses get more out of the visitors they already have and spend far less time figuring out how to capitalize on their audiences. The startup said in the press release that its AI-powered testing platform has helped customers generate more than $300 million in additional revenue, with conversion rates improving by more than 20% on average.

Coframe’s customers include Dropbox, BCG, Block, Backcountry and Sur La Table.

Speed is another part of the startup’s appeal. Coframe said it built and launched an experiment for Dropbox in 30 minutes that previously would have required four weeks of engineering, design and quality-assurance work. For an unnamed Fortune 500 customer, the company said it launched dozens of variations and increased conversions by 70% within 30 days of the contract being signed.

The idea is to let businesses try more approaches without adding weeks of production work each time. Faster testing can help teams identify promising changes and move past unsuccessful ones sooner.

Khosla sees AI as a way to deliver an experience built around each visitor to a website, like Coframe is doing.

“The alternative is to write code for every page that’s presented, but that’s too expensive and way too slow,” Khosla told Business Insider. “This idea of a customized page for every user is a really good idea.”

He made billions betting on what’s next. Now this 71-year-old investor’s latest move reflects his conviction that AI will change how the Internet works.

The Rings Of Power Season 3 Review: Prime Video's Lord Of The Rings Series Still Falls Short

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

If there's one theme the latest installment of "The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power" highlights above the rest, it's this: Time comes for us all. Whether it's the glacial pace experienced by immortal Elves or the nonstop blur the rest of Middle-earth's mere mortals have been doomed (or gifted?) to receive, this army's inevitable march is one that nobody — not even a Dark Lord — can hope to defeat.

Four years after the Prime Video series first made waves among a passionate, if divisive fanbase, it's clear co-showrunners J.D. Payne and Patrick McKay have felt this pressure as keenly as anyone. An up-and-down debut season with glimpses of potential eventually found itself surpassed by a sophomore effort that went darker, bolder, and better than before. But, to date, fans have been left waiting for the moment when this J.R.R. Tolkien adaptation truly comes into its own.

Time, unfortunately, is a commodity that "The Rings of Power" doesn't have on its side this year. There's Amazon's head-scratching release strategy that (mostly) becomes clear in retrospect, with eight episodes of streaming television squeezed into three total weeks. More troublingly, the actual story does itself very few favors. There are simply too many far-flung locations to check in on, too many main characters and uninteresting supporting players to service, too much nerdy lore for casuals to digest (if any are even left) ... and, despite some of the most sluggish pacing in streaming, there's hardly enough space to let them breathe. It's an issue that rears its ugly, Balrog-shaped head again and again.

In short, Season 3 is an encapsulation of "The Rings of Power" as a whole: some incredibly high highs, several frustrating lows, and the creeping feeling that time is finally starting to run out.

Season 3's big claim to fame has been heavily teased as the creation of the legendary One Ring, which is the one doomsday event overshadowing over every single storyline this year. Five years after the conclusion of Season 2, as Galadriel's exposition-dump narration explains in the premiere, Sauron's armies wage war on the rest of Middle-earth. Only the Elves and their magical, untarnished rings remain capable of holding out. But when a suspicious Orc arrives (Adam Young as Marnûkh, easily one of the season's best new additions) bearing game-changing news of Sauron's plans, this kicks off a series of events that will either lead to good's greatest triumph over evil ... or as stunning a failure as anything in Tolkien's legendarium.

As a prequel, of course, "The Rings of Power" uses our knowledge of what's to come in "The Lord of the Rings" to its advantage. As a result, every moment Sauron is on screen trying (and failing) to forge his superweapon to rule them all absolutely soars. Whether he's cruelly bossing powerless underlings around, casually committing murder, arguing with the ghosts in his head, or generally sulking over his inability to seduce Galadriel to his cause, Charlie Vickers's Sauron will have you rooting for the villain every step of the way. He brings a delightful sense of theatricality and purpose to every evil act, while the long-awaited introduction of Galadriel's MIA husband Celeborn (Jamie Campbell Bower, another major highlight of Season 3) finally provides the third point of the unlikeliest love triangle in the canon.

But if Sauron is the main driving force behind the best narrative arc of them all, "The Rings of Power" fares far worse everywhere else.

This "Lord of the Rings" series has always had trouble finding the right balance among its varying subplots, separated by narrative relevance and theme just as much as by distance. Nowhere does that feel more glaring than in Season 3. The courtly intrigue and succession struggles taking place in the Dwarven realm of Khazad-dûm aim for "House of the Dragon"-like dramatics, but neither of the plucky performances by Owain Arthur's King Durin or Sophia Nomvete's Queen Disa can make this feel like anything less than a misguided detour.

Similarly, the brewing civil war in Númenór takes ages to click into place (and, even then, not because of any established locals, but because Robert Aramayo's ever-conflicted Elf Elrond finally gets involved). And don't forget there's another B-plot entirely in the desert of Rhûn, with Daniel Weyman's Gandalf and Ciarán Hinds' Dark Wizard circling one another.

The further we get from Sauron's antics in Mordor, the less effective "The Rings of Power" undeniably becomes.

The five-year time jump should've allowed for J.D Payne, Patrick McKay, and co-writer Justin Doble to regroup strategically and reset the creative board a bit. And, to an extent, they do manage to cut things down to a more manageable size. A major character death or two helps trim an unwieldy ensemble that has only grown more so over the years, while others undergo some overdo changes in both personality and role. Even the lovable, pint-sized Harfoots have been written out completely this time around. But these are half-measures, at best. As this saga hits its midway point, viewers will be left with the impression that merely reshuffling the deck wasn't nearly enough.

These structural issues extend to the main conflict of Season 3, as well. In the early episodes, the creative team struggles with establishing the stakes of such an abstract threat as the One Ring. Season 1 had the (seemingly) unseen menace of Sauron working in its favor, while Season 2 organically built up to the epic siege of Eregion and the manipulation of Celebrimbor as its big emotional payoff. Season 3 pays lip service to the idea of Mordor's forces on the brink of total victory; but, other than a few minor skirmishes here and the aftermath of some battles there, we barely actually see this for ourselves.

It's a shame because, creatively and technically, the most expensive series on streaming has never looked better nor felt more cinematic. Directors Charlotte Brändström, Sanaa Hamri, and Stefan Schwartz depict a breathtaking level of scope and scale that modern viewers have almost become inured to these days. Together, J.D. Payne and Patrick McKay dream up some of the most spectacular fantasy concepts Tolkien nerds could only ever dare to imagine. As of the seven episodes out of eight made available for critics to review, it all builds up to the moment we've all been waiting for — one that, for a moment, absolutely lives up to the hype.

Is that enough? "Always is a long time," Galadriel suggests with a twinkle in her eye early on. With its five-season plan hopefully still in place, "The Rings of Power" doesn't have anywhere near that long to make up for lost time.

"The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power" Season 3 premieres with four episodes November 11, 2026 on Prime Video.

Top 12 Crypto Debit Cards to Consider

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Home » Cryptopedia » Top 12 Crypto Debit Cards to Consider

Compare fees, cashback, availability, and features of 12 crypto cards to find the right option for your spending needs.

Crypto debit cards are no longer just a way to convert Bitcoin into cash for everyday purchases. In 2026, there are plenty of options, from exchange-linked cards and stablecoin payment solutions to self-custody cards connected directly to crypto wallets.

But choosing one isn’t just about cashback. Availability depends on your country, better rewards may require staking or a paid subscription, and fees can quickly eat into your benefits. It’s also worth checking how each card works, as some are prepaid debit cards while others offer credit backed by crypto holdings.

We’ve selected 12 crypto cards worth considering in 2026, comparing their features, fees, rewards, and availability to help you find the right option, whether you prioritize low fees, stablecoin spending, cashback, or control over your assets.

The Coinbase Card Visa prepaid debit card that lets US customers (excluding Hawaii) spend directly from their Coinbase accounts.

It is a custodial card, meaning funds remain in your Coinbase account and are deducted when you make a purchase. Coinbase automatically converts crypto to USD at checkout or for ATM withdrawals, so merchants never receive crypto. You also get a virtual card as soon as you’re approved, with Apple Pay and Google Pay support.

Fees are relatively low. There are no annual fees or charges for spending USD or crypto, including USDC. Coinbase Card does not charge monthly or foreign transaction fees, and there are no Coinbase ATM withdrawal fees (though ATM operators may charge their own). The main cost is Coinbase’s crypto conversion spread. Spending USD or USDC avoids it, making them the cheapest funding options.

Crypto rewards are the main attraction, but rates vary. Rewards are optional, available to US users, and paid in a crypto asset of your choice from a rotating list. Rates are displayed in the app, can be changed at any time, and have no stated cap. Although many reviews advertise rates of up to 4%, Coinbase does not guarantee a fixed rate on its official card page. Check the current in-app offer before applying.

Best for: US-based Coinbase users who want a simple, low-fee card and crypto rewards without a credit check.

Crypto.com’s crypto debit card depends on your region. In the US, it is now a Visa Signature credit card issued by Comenity Capital Bank through Bread Financial. Cardholders can earn rewards in BTC or CRO, but approval requires a credit check. Applications are made through the Crypto.com app.

Rewards range from roughly 1.5% to 5–6%, depending on the tier. However, higher Level Up tiers require locking up or staking CRO. Those who don’t want to commit CRO can choose the entry tier.

Outside the US, the card remains a prepaid debit card. In the European Economic Area (EEA), the free tier offers no cashback, while the Plus plan (€4.99/month) starts at 2% cashback.

Best for: Users who already hold CRO and want to maximize rewards.

KAST is a Visa debit card that that spends a USD balance funded with stablecoins. It offers three tiers: Standard (free, 1.5% cashback), Premium ($1000 a year, 2% cashback) and Private ($10,000 a year, 3%).

Cashback is paid in dollars, rather than tokens. Monthly cashback limits apply: $2,000 in spend for Standard, $10,000 for Premium and $40,000 for Private.

However, KAST fees can reduce the returns. Foreign exchange fees on KAS crypto debit card range from 0.5% to 1.75%, while a physical Standard card costs $40 to deliver.

KAST estimates Standard users may keep up to 1% cashback after FX fees. Premium and Private also include a Visa Infinite card and KAST Points on eligible spending.

Best for: Fequent stablecoin spenders, especially those spending in USD.

Krak, Kraken’s money app, launched its Visa debit card in the US in August 2026. Available as a physical and virtual card, it lets users spend from 600+ fiat and crypto assets, with purchases converted to USD at checkout. The card is issued by Lead Bank and powered by Stripe Issuing.

Krak offers up to 2% cashback in USD or Bitcoin, paid after transactions settle, with no monthly or annual fees.

Rewards depend on users’ average asset balances across Krak, Kraken and Kraken Pro. The card is available in all US states except New York, Maine, Massachusetts and Indiana. Crypto-funded purchases may incur conversion spreads and trigger taxable events.

Best for: US users who hold a mix of crypto and cash and want one card for everything.

The MetaMask Card lets users spend directly from their self-custody wallet, without preloading funds. Built with Mastercard and Baanx (now Monavate) and issued by Cross River Bank in the US, it works wherever Mastercard is accepted, including online, in-store and through Apple Pay or Google Pay. Unlike exchange cards, MetaMask Card allows to retain full control of your crypto until purchase.

The card has two tiers: the standard card offers 1% cashback in mUSD, while the Metal card costs $199/year and offers 3% cashback on up to $10,000 in annual spending, no foreign transaction fees and higher limits.

Outside the US, it is available in Argentina, Brazil, Canada, Colombia, the EEA, Mexico, Switzerland and the UK. The trade-off is that users take full responsibility for securing their own assets.

Best for: Existing MetaMask users who want to keep control of their keys.

The Nexo Card is a Mastercard issued by DiPocket UAB, a Lithuanian-licensed e-money institution (EMI). It offers two app-controlled modes: Credit Mode, which lets users borrow against their crypto without selling it, and Debit Mode.

Cashback depends on Nexo’s loyalty tier, portfolio balance, and card mode. Rewards are paid in NEXO tokens or BTC, with payouts changeable at any time. Rates can reach 2% in NEXO or 0.5% in BTC, although Nexo’s regional page lists up to 2% cashback for credit card holders.

The card is available to eligible users in selected European markets, including the EEA and UK.

Best for: European long-term holders who want liquidity without selling.

The Bybit Card is a Mastercard debit card linked to your Bybit account. In the EEA and Switzerland, it is issued by Moorwand Ltd and Harmoniie SAS, with a 0.5% FX fee on top of Mastercard’s rate and a 0.9% crypto conversion fee.

Availability is limited to selected markets, including the EEA, Switzerland, Australia, Argentina, Brazil, parts of Asia Pacific, and Mexico.

Rewards depend on spending tiers or VIP status, not a flat cashback rate. The advertised “up to 10%” applies only to top tiers.

Fees and limits vary by region, and cashback without VIP status can be minimal, particularly outside Europe. US users should consider alternatives.

Best for: Non-US Bybit traders who want to spend without leaving the crypto exchange.

RedotPay is a custodial Visa card for global stablecoin users, allowing crypto spending in over 100 countries.

It costs $10 for a virtual card or $100 for a physical one, with no monthly or annual fees, and supports major assets including USDT, USDC, BTC, ETH, BNB and SOL.

Standard pricing includes a 1% crypto-to-fiat conversion fee for USD transactions and an additional 1.2% FX markup.

Standard RedotPay accounts offer no ongoing cashback, relying on welcome bonuses and referral rewards. However, its optional RedotPay Pro membership offers 3% cashback on eligible Apple Pay and Google Pay purchases.

The card is unavailable in the US, Canada, China, Japan, South Korea, the Philippines and Russia.

Best for: Freelancers and remote workers paid in USDT outside the US.

Uphold relaunched its US debit card in October 2025, advertising up to 6% in XRP rewards, with additional benefits for direct deposit users. Actual rates vary by tier and conditions, with some sources citing up to 4%. The US offers Essential and Elite plans, both paying rewards exclusively in XRP.

Essential has no annual fee but charges $4.99 for a physical card, $2.95 per ATM withdrawal, and $1.50 in foreign transaction fees.

Elite costs $99.99 annually, includes a metal card, and waives Uphold ATM and foreign transaction fees.

Crypto-funded spending may involve spreads and taxable events. Availability varies by state, while UK users receive a separate program offering 1% cashback in GBP.

Best for: US users who want a multi-asset card and are comfortable earning XRP.

Ether.fi Cash is a non-custodial Visa card that connects DeFi assets to everyday spending. Users can pay directly with USDC or LiquidUSD, or borrow against their crypto without selling it.

The card combines debit-style direct payments with crypto-backed credit. Rewards reach 3% in USDC for most users and 4% for VIP members on the first $50,000.

It offers up to 3% cashback in USDC for most users, rising to 4% for VIP members on the first $50,000 spent. Fees and eligibility vary by tier and region, with some benefits requiring ETHFI staking.

Best for: experienced DeFi users already inside the ether.fi ecosystem.

The Bitpanda Card is a simple Visa debit card for EEA residents. It is available in all Eurozone countries except Cyprus, and requires a verified Bitpanda account and the mobile app.

The card offers 1% cashback on eligible crypto spending, access to more than 600 crypto assets, no monthly fee and no FX fees charged by Bitpanda, although Visa mark-ups may apply.

There are a few important exclusions: stablecoins, precious metals, Bitpanda Stocks and fiat balances do not qualify for cashback. Each payment also triggers a trade, meaning standard trading fees apply. ATM withdrawals cost 2%, with a minimum fee of €2.

Best for: Euro-area users who already invest with Bitpanda.

Gate Card is a custodial crypto debit card linked to your Gate account, available in virtual and physical Visa or Mastercard versions, depending on region. Compatible cards support Apple Pay and Google Pay, with instant virtual issuance.

Cashback ranges from 1% to 8%, depending on your VIP tier or monthly spending. Rewards are issued as points redeemable for USDT, GT and other supported assets, subject to monthly limits of $5–$400.

Fees vary by card, but the latest Blue and AI versions offer free issuance and no monthly fee, with a 0.9% top-up fee and 1.5–1.7% FX fee. Their limits reach $150,000 daily and $1 million monthly.

Level 2 KYC is required. The international service excludes certain jurisdictions, including the US, where a separate Gate US Visa card is available under different terms.

Best for: Existing Gate users who want crypto spending with tier-based rewards and are comfortable keeping funds on a custodial exchange.

The best crypto debit card depends on how you plan to use it, where you live, and which features matter most. Before applying, consider these five factors:

Not every crypto card works worldwide. Some are restricted to the US, EEA, UK, or selected countries. Check whether the provider supports residents of your country and whether the card offers the same features in your region.

Look beyond monthly and annual fees. Crypto conversion spreads, foreign exchange markups, ATM withdrawal charges, and card issuance costs can all affect how much you actually spend.

Compare the rewards you can realistically earn, not just the highest advertised percentage. Some providers require staking, paid subscriptions, or minimum spending levels to unlock better rates. Also check whether rewards are paid in cash, stablecoins, or volatile crypto assets.

Exchange-linked cards generally rely on custodial accounts, while self-custody products can allow users to retain control of their assets until payment. Understand who controls your funds and what happens if your account or wallet is compromised.

Some crypto cards offer prepaid spending, while others provide credit backed by crypto collateral. These products have different risks, repayment requirements, and potential tax implications. Make sure you understand which type you are applying for.

Traditional debit cards use money held in a bank account, while crypto debit cards are linked to cryptocurrency exchanges, digital wallets or stablecoin balances.

When making a payment, some crypto cards automatically convert your digital assets into fiat currency. Others let you spend stablecoins directly or borrow against your crypto holdings.

The main differences come down to funding, custody, fees and rewards. Crypto cards can offer cashback in digital assets and easier access to crypto funds, but may also involve conversion fees, additional account requirements and tax implications.

In some jurisdictions, spending crypto is treated as a taxable disposal. It’s worth checking the local tax rules before using crypto for everyday payments.

Crypto debit cards make it easier to use digital assets for everyday purchases, whether through crypto-funded payments, stablecoin spending or cashback rewards.

But the benefits aren’t always as good as they look. Conversion spreads, foreign transaction fees, subscription costs and staking requirements can eat into your rewards.

The best card depends on how you plan to use it. If low fees matter most, an exchange-linked card may be enough. If you prefer greater control over your assets or want to spend stablecoins, other options may be more suitable. For cashback, look beyond the advertised rate and factor in all associated costs.

Ultimately, choose a card that fits your spending habits, location and risk tolerance. Check the latest fees and terms, and don’t overlook potential tax implications.

A crypto debit card is a payment card linked to a cryptocurrency exchange account or digital wallet. It allows users to make purchases at merchants that accept its payment network, with crypto or stablecoins converted into fiat currency when required.

No. Availability depends on the card issuer, payment provider, and local regulations. Some cards are restricted to US residents, while others support selected European, Asian, or Latin American markets. Features and fees may also differ between regions.

Some crypto debit cards have no annual or monthly fees, but other costs may apply. These include crypto conversion spreads, foreign exchange fees, ATM withdrawal charges, issuance fees, and paid membership subscriptions.

It depends on the card. Many custodial cards convert cryptocurrency into fiat currency when a payment is processed. Some self-custody cards allow users to retain control of their assets until the transaction takes place, while crypto-backed credit products may allow spending without an immediate sale.

Tax treatment depends on your country and the type of reward. Spending appreciated cryptocurrency may trigger a taxable disposal, while cashback and promotional rewards can have different tax treatment. Consult the applicable tax rules in your jurisdiction.
